abstract_pt: 'As doenças respiratórias são uma das principais causas de morbilidade
  e mortalidade, sendo responsáveis pela morte de milhões de pessoas em todo o mundo
  anualmente. As doenças pulmonares obstrutivas representam um desafio crescente para
  a saúde pública, atendendo à sua elevada prevalência e impacto na saúde populacional,
  bem como aos elevados custos socioeconómicos associados. Apesar da morbimortalidade
  associada às doenças respiratórias crónicas, as opções terapêuticas permanecem limitadas,
  o que se deve, essencialmente, ao desconhecimento dos mecanismos fisiopatológicos
  subjacentes. Por conseguinte, o estudo das doenças respiratórias assume um papel
  fundamental no desenvolvimento de métodos de diagnóstico precoce e na otimização
  de protocolos de tratamento, que permitam reduzir o impacto global associado a esta
  patologia.O principal objetivo desta tese foi o desenvolvimento de algoritmos que
  contribuam para uma melhor acuidade diagnóstica de várias patologias respiratórias,
  bem como para a monitorização da saúde e estado geral do sistema respiratório. Para
  isto, foram utilizados dois biossinais, nomeadamente som respiratório e Tomografia
  de Impedância Elétrica (TIE). Foram desenvolvidas várias metodologias baseadas em
  frameworks de aprendizagem automática nos seguintes tópicos: (1) análise e classificação
  automática de Sons Respiratórios Adventícios (SRA), (2) diagnóstico diferencial
  de doenças respiratórias e (3) estimativa adimensional do fluxo respiratório. No
  que diz respeito ao primeiro tópico, propusemos vários modelos de aprendizagem automática
  para a análise automática de SRA, com especial destaque para a classificação estática
  de eventos de SRA e classificação de SRA em gravações completas de som respiratório.
  Em ambas as tarefas, desenvolvemos dois modelos de aprendizagem profunda (DL) baseados
  numa arquitetura de dois inputs que combinam diferentes tipos de dados: no primeiro
  modelo, combinámos duas representações tempo-frequência, espectrogramas e mel-espectrogramas;
  enquanto que no segundo modelo combinámos sinais de áudio no domínio temporal com
  espectrogramas. O primeiro modelo, que combina duas representações tempo-frequência,
  foi utilizado para a classificação estática de SRA, nomeadamente sibilos, fervores
  e outros eventos, com a RSD. O modelo atingiu uma accuracy global de 81,8%. O segundo
  modelo foi desenvolvido no âmbito do IEEE BioCAS 2023 Grand Challenge, onde haviam
  várias tarefas de classificação de eventos de SRA estáticos e de classificação de
  SRA em gravações completas de som respiratório. O modelo obteve o terceiro lugar
  na competição, com uma pontuação total de 0.5903. Relativamente ao segundo tópico,
  compilámos a primeira base de dados open-access bimodal para o diagnóstico diferencial
  de doenças respiratórias (BRACETS). Esta base de dados contém dados de 78 sujeitos
  com gravações de sons respiratórios como dados de TIE. Para além da publicação do
  repositório, propusemos também uma framework baseada em aprendizagem automática
  para a classificação de doenças respiratórias. O modelo com melhor desempenho atingiu
  uma balanced accuracy de 38.6±13.1% para classificação ao nível das amostras e de
  50.8±10.6% para classificação ao nível de sujeitos. Por último, propusemos um novo
  conceito de fluxo respiratório baseado no Curva Global de Impedância (CGI) da TIE,
  o fluxo respiratório adimensional. Propusemos um modelo híbrido de aprendizagem
  automática profunda (CNN + LSTM) para extrair características dos sons respiratórios
  e modelar as suas dependências temporais. Em seguida, utilizámos uma abordagem de
  ensemble para obter o fluxo respiratório para gravações de som respiratório completas.
  O modelo alcançou um erro absoluto médio de 0.134±0.061. Em suma, este trabalho
  contribuiu para avanços significativos em diversas áreas relacionadas com a avaliação
  automática da função respiratória, conduzindo a uma melhoria na rapidez e eficácia
  na deteção e gestão de doenças respiratórias, principalmente através do desenvolvimento
  de métodos de aprendizagem automática para o processamento de biosinais. Além disso,
  os resultados deste trabalho poderão também contribuir para o desenvolvimento de
  dispositivos wearables avançados que integrem os algoritmos desenvolvidos. Como
  objetivo unificador final desta tese, visamos o uso destes algoritmos em dispositivos
  wearables capazes de realizar gravações em ambientes remotos ou clínicos, processar
  automaticamente os dados recolhidos e gerar informações clinicamente relevantes.
  Embora a integração destes algoritmos num dispositivo wearable real não constituísse
  um objetivo desta tese, a sua implementação futura poderá viabilizar uma monitorização
  contínua e em tempo real, beneficiando tanto pacientes com doenças respiratórias
  crónicas como pessoas em áreas com recursos limitados. À medida que a tecnologia
  evolui, este tipo de sistemas integrados de monitorização respiratória tornar-se-ão
  essenciais para cuidados preventivos e diagnóstico precoce.'
abstract_en: 'Respiratory diseases are a leading cause of mortality and morbidity
  worldwide, with chronic and acute respiratory conditions contributing to millions
  of deaths each year. Obstructive lung diseases pose a growing challenge to public
  health due to their high prevalence, significant morbidity and mortality, and substantial
  socioeconomic costs. Despite the increasing burden of chronic respiratory diseases,
  treatment options are limited. This is mainly due to a lack of understanding of
  the underlying mechanisms behind such diseases and their chronic nature. For all
  the reasons mentioned above, studying respiratory diseases is essential to developing
  early detection methods, improving treatment protocols, and ultimately reducing
  the global burden of respiratory-related mortality and morbidity. In this research,
  our primary goal was the development of algorithms that contribute to better diagnosing
  several respiratory conditions and monitoring overall respiratory health and status,
  using both respiratory sound and Electrical Impedance Tomography (EIT) data. In
  particular, we have developed several methodologies based on machine learning frameworks
  in the following topics: (1) automated analysis and classification of Adventitious
  Respiratory Sounds (ARS), (2) differential diagnosis of respiratory diseases, and
  (3) dimensionless respiratory airflow estimation. Considering the first topic, we
  proposed several state-of-the-art machine learning pipelines for the automated analysis
  of ARS, especially focusing on ARS static event classification and ARS classification.
  For these tasks, we developed two Deep Learning (DL) models based on a dual-input
  architecture that combines different types of data: one model incorporates spectrograms
  and melspectrograms, while the other combines raw audio signals with spectrograms.
  The first model with the combination of the two time-frequency representations was
  used for the static classification of ARS, namely wheezes, crackles, and other events
  with the Respiratory Sound Database (RSD). The model achieved an overall accuracy
  of 81.8%. The second model was developed for the IEEE BioCAS 2023 Grand Challenge,
  where there were ARS static event classification and ARS classification tasks. The
  model placed third in the competition with a total score of 0.5903. Regarding the
  second topic, we have compiled the first bimodal open-access database (Bimodal Repository
  of Auscultation Coupled with Electrical Impedance Thoracic Signals (BRACETS)), which
  includes data from 78 subjects for the differential diagnosis of respiratory diseases.
  This database contains both respiratory sound recordings and EIT data. We proposed
  a machine-learning-based pipeline for classifying this data, and the best-performing
  classification model achieved a balanced accuracy of 38.6±13.1% for sample-level
  classification and 50.8±10.6% for subject-level classification. Finally, we proposed
  a new concept of respiratory airflow based on the EIT Global Impedance Curve (GIC),
  the dimensionless respiratory airflow. We proposed a hybrid DL model (CNN + LSTM)
  to extract features from the respiratory sound and model their temporal dependencies.
  Then, we used an ensemble approach to combine multiple outputs of our model and
  obtain the respiratory airflow waveform for entire respiratory audio signals as
  the final output. The model achieved a mean absolute error of 0.134±0.061. In summary,
  this work contributes to advancing the field of automated respiratory function assessment,
  enhancing the speed and effectiveness of disease detection and management, mainly
  through developing machine learning methods for processing biosignals (respiratory
  sound and EIT). In addition, this work might contribute to the development of advanced
  wearable devices, integrating the developed algorithms. As the ultimate unifying
  goal, we envision the deployment of the developed algorithms in wearable devices
  capable of recording in remote or clinical settings, automatically processing the
  recorded data, and generating relevant insights. Although the integration of such
  algorithms in a real wearable device was not performed throughout this thesis, deploying
  these technologies in wearables could possibly enable continuous, real-time monitoring,
  benefiting patients with chronic respiratory conditions and those in resource-limited
  areas. As the technology evolves, integrated respiratory monitoring systems will
  become essential for preventive care, early diagnosis, and personalized treatment
  of respiratory diseases.'

Abstract
R
espiratory diseases are a leading cause of mortality and mor-
bidity worldwide, with chronic and acute respiratory conditions
contributing to millions of deaths each year. Obstructive lung
diseases pose a growing challenge to public health due to their
high prevalence, significant morbidity and mortality, and substantial socioeconomic
costs. Despite the increasing burden of chronic respiratory diseases, treatment op-
tions are limited. This is mainly due to a lack of understanding of the underlying
mechanisms behind such diseases and their chronic nature. For all the reasons men-
tioned above, studying respiratory diseases is essential to developing early detection
methods, improving treatment protocols, and ultimately reducing the global burden
of respiratory-related mortality and morbidity.
In this research, our primary goal was the development of algorithms that con-
tribute to better diagnosing several respiratory conditions and monitoring overall res-
piratory health and status, using both respiratory sound and Electrical Impedance
Tomography (EIT) data. In particular, we have developed several methodologies
based on machine learning frameworks in the following topics: (1) automated anal-
ysis and classification of Adventitious Respiratory Sounds (ARS), (2) differential
diagnosis of respiratory diseases, and (3) dimensionless respiratory airflow estima-
tion.
Considering the first topic, we proposed several state-of-the-art machine learn-
ing pipelines for the automated analysis of ARS, especially focusing on ARS static
event classification and ARS classification. For these tasks, we developed two Deep
Learning (DL) models based on a dual-input architecture that combines different
types of data: one model incorporates spectrograms and mel-spectrograms, while
v

the other combines raw audio signals with spectrograms. The first model with the
combination of the two time-frequency representations was used for the static clas-
sification of ARS, namely wheezes, crackles, and other events with the Respiratory
Sound Database (RSD). The model achieved an overall accuracy of 81.8%. The
second model was developed for the IEEE BioCAS 2023 Grand Challenge, where
there were ARS static event classification and ARS classification tasks. The model
placed third in the competition with a total score of 0.5903.
Regarding the second topic, we have compiled the first bimodal open-access
database (Bimodal Repository of Auscultation Coupled with Electrical Impedance
Thoracic Signals (BRACETS)), which includes data from 78 subjects for the dif-
ferential diagnosis of respiratory diseases. This database contains both respiratory
sound recordings and EIT data. We proposed a machine-learning-based pipeline
for classifying this data, and the best-performing classification model achieved a
balanced accuracy of 38.6 ±13.1% for sample-level classification and 50.8 ±10.6% for
subject-level classification.
Finally, we proposed a new concept of respiratory airflow based on the EIT
Global Impedance Curve (GIC), the dimensionless respiratory airflow. We proposed
a hybrid DL model (CNN + LSTM) to extract features from the respiratory sound
and model their temporal dependencies. Then, we used an ensemble approach to
combine multiple outputs of our model and obtain the respiratory airflow waveform
for entire respiratory audio signals as the final output. The model achieved a mean
absolute error of 0.134 ±0.061.
In summary, this work contributes to advancing the field of automated respira-
tory function assessment, enhancing the speed and effectiveness of disease detection
and management, mainly through developing machine learning methods for process-
ing biosignals (respiratory sound and EIT). In addition, this work might contribute
to the development of advanced wearable devices, integrating the developed algo-
rithms. As the ultimate unifying goal, we envision the deployment of the developed
algorithms in wearable devices capable of recording in remote or clinical settings,
automatically processing the recorded data, and generating relevant insights. Al-
though the integration of such algorithms in a real wearable device was not performed
throughout this thesis, deploying these technologies in wearables could possibly en-
able continuous, real-time monitoring, benefiting patients with chronic respiratory
vi

conditions and those in resource-limited areas. As the technology evolves, integrated
respiratory monitoring systems will become essential for preventive care, early diag-
nosis, and personalized treatment of respiratory diseases.
Keywords: Respiratory Diseases, Respiratory Sound, Electrical Impedance Tomog-
raphy, Automated Respiratory Function Assessment, Respiratory Dis-
ease Database, Differential Diagnosis of Respiratory Diseases, Adven-
titious Respiratory Sounds, Respiratory Airflow
vii

T
he development of methods for the automatic monitoring of respiratory
function has been an active area of research over the past few decades.
Recently, a significant push has been made towards developing auto-
mated methodologies for respiratory function monitoring, especially
using respiratory sound and Electrical Impedance Tomography (EIT). However,
automatic systems capable of diagnosing and continuously monitoring respiratory
patients in hospital and home settings are yet to become a reality. This chapter
introduces the main motivations behind the need to develop such methodologies for
the automated monitoring of respiratory function. In addition, the main goals and
contributions of this thesis are presented, as well as the primary scientific outcomes
derived from the current work.
1.1 Motivation and Context
Respiratory diseases represent one of the biggest health concerns around the globe,
causing one-sixth of all deaths worldwide. The impact of these remains as significant
today as it was at the turn of the century and will likely remain so for several decades
to come. In 2013, it was estimated that each year in the European Union, one-eight of
all deaths are due to respiratory diseases, and lung conditions cause at least 6 million
hospital admissions [1]. According to a study presented by the European Union’s
1

2 CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
Statistical Office (Eurostat), in 2016, 339000 deaths occurred in the European Union
due to respiratory diseases, accounting for 7.5% of the total fatalities. Furthermore,
it reveals that in 2017, respiratory diseases were responsible for at least one in
every ten deaths in countries such as Portugal, Spain, Belgium, Greece, Malta, and
others. In Portugal, the National Institute of Statistics has reported that, in 2022,
respiratory disease deaths (excluding COVID-19) increased by 18.1% in comparison
to the previous year, killing more than 12000 people [2]. In addition, worldwide,
respiratory diseases, which include Chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD),
Lower Respiratory Tract Infections (LRTI), trachea, bronchus and lung cancer, and
tuberculosis, were among the top 10 causes of death in 2021, killing millions of
people each year [3]. Figure 1.1 presents the top ten causes of death worldwide in
2021, reported by the World Health Organization (WHO).

Figure 1.1: Top 10 global causes of death worldwide (image from [3]).
Besides their high prevalence and mortality, respiratory diseases also have a
high social and economic impact worldwide. They are estimated to have a financial

1.1. MOTIV ATION AND CONTEXT 3
burden on the healthcare systems and society of over 300 billion only in the European
Union, with an increasing trend [1, 4]. The chronic nature of many respiratory
diseases imposes long-term challenges for patients, as they often require ongoing
medical management, frequent hospitalizations, and complex treatment regimens.
The emotional, physical, and financial strain of these diseases further exacerbates
the overall burden on patients and healthcare systems.
In addition to the high costs associated with healthcare systems, these conditions
severely impact a person’s quality of life, causing symptoms such as breathlessness,
chronic coughing, wheezing, and fatigue. In advanced stages, respiratory diseases
can also lead to a progressive decline in lung function, reduced physical activity, and
an increased risk of life-threatening complications, such as respiratory failure. For
instance, COPD is typically a progressive disease, meaning that the patient’s condi-
tion gradually worsens as well as the symptoms experienced. They may also suffer
from high levels of morbidity, leading to a substantial decline in their quality of life,
as daily activities become severely compromised [5]. Nevertheless, the progression
rate of such chronic diseases can be reduced with treatment, even though none of
the existing medications has been shown to modify the long-term decline in lung
function [6]. Therefore, significant research efforts have been dedicated to improv-
ing early diagnosis and routine monitoring of patients with respiratory diseases to
allow for timely interventions and increase patient’s life expectancy and quality of
life [7].
The complexity and prevalence of respiratory diseases underline the urgent need
for more effective monitoring and diagnosis methods. Traditional diagnostic tools,
such as auscultation, spirometry, and medical imaging, rely heavily on the expertise
of clinicians and are often limited to clinical settings. However, respiratory diseases
often fluctuate in severity, with exacerbations and subtle symptoms that may go
undetected between doctor visits. Such slight changes present a significant chal-
lenge for clinicians who rely on sparse data points to make accurate diagnoses and
treatment decisions. Based on the reasons above, the current diagnostic methods
can yield subjective and inconsistent evaluations of the patients.
In response to these challenges, there is growing interest in developing auto-
mated methodologies to assist clinicians in monitoring and diagnosing respiratory
diseases. By leveraging advancements in signal processing, machine learning, and
sensing wearable technology, automated systems offer the potential to provide con-
tinuous, real-time assessment of respiratory health and function. These systems
can help detect early signs of deterioration, track disease progression, and provide
objective, consistent measurements, reducing the reliance on sparse clinical evalu-
ations and paving the way for more objective monitoring. For patients, deploying
automated monitoring technologies in wearable or home-based systems can enable
more proactive management of their conditions, reducing the frequency of hospi-
tal visits and improving overall quality of life. These technologies can serve as

4 CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
valuable decision-support tools for healthcare providers, improving diagnostic accu-
racy, optimizing treatment plans, and ultimately enhancing patient outcomes. The
development of automated technologies for monitoring and diagnosing respiratory
diseases is particularly significant in the context of a pandemic such as COVID-19
due to the widespread and severe impact that respiratory viruses can have on global
health systems. During a pandemic, reducing the burden on healthcare systems
is essential, facilitating early detection and intervention and enabling safe remote
monitoring by protecting healthcare workers from exposure.
1.2 Goals and Contributions
Aiming at a push towards the development of methodologies that can be deployed
for the automated monitoring of respiratory function, the main objective of this
thesis is the development of algorithms that contribute to better diagnosing several
respiratory conditions and monitoring overall respiratory health and status, using
both respiratory sound and EIT data. Overall respiratory health refers to the condi-
tion and functionality of the respiratory system, which includes the lungs, airways,
and associated musculature, as well as its ability to efficiently exchange oxygen and
carbon dioxide to support the body’s metabolic demands. A healthy respiratory
system typically demonstrates optimal lung capacity, clear airways, effective gas ex-
change, and minimal signs of inflammation or obstruction. Such function can be
evaluated by the presence/absence of respiratory pathologies, presence/absence of
Adventitious Respiratory Sounds (ARS), and respiration patterns. In broader terms,
the main research questions guiding this thesis can be summarized as follows:
• Can respiratory function and overall respiratory health be monitored through
automated machine-learning-based methodologies using respiratory sound and
EIT?
• Is there an added benefit in a multimodal approach to assess respiratory function
and overall respiratory health by combining respiratory sound and EIT?
By leveraging the use of machine learning frameworks, the current thesis aims
to create models capable of identifying and classifying critical respiratory biomark-
ers, such as ARS, airflow patterns, and develop generalized methodologies for the
differential diagnosis of respiratory diseases, which are essential for diagnosing and
monitoring respiratory conditions.
Ultimately, this work seeks to provide a framework for continuous, automated
respiratory monitoring that could improve early detection, support clinical decision-
making, and enable more personalized management of respiratory diseases, espe-
cially in remote and low-resource settings. In fact, such framework has as a primary
deployment target a wearable device capable of recording biosignals, namely respi-
ratory sound and EIT, such as the vest developed in the Wearable Electronics for

1.3. WELMO PROJECT 5
Effective Lung Monitoring (WELMO) project, which is briefly described in the next
section (section 1.3).
1.3 WELMO Project
The current thesis was developed within the scope of the WELMO project. The
developed methodologies were mainly aligned with the objectives of the project. The
EU-funded WELMO consortium developed a new generation of low-cost and low-
power miniaturized sensors integrated into a comfortable vest, enabling the effective
and accurate remote monitoring of the lungs through the collection of respiration
sound over multiple locations of the chest and EIT signals. WELMO’s proposition
offers continuous monitoring and functional imaging, unraveling information at the
regional lung level and improving patient assessment. Patient safety is also enhanced
since the techniques employed are non-invasive and do not rely on ionizing radiation.
WELMO, as a wearable solution, improves patients’ quality of life by contributing to
the transition from hospital to home care and helps release the burden on healthcare
facilities.
Figure 1.2 presents the developed wearable vest and the sensors used for recording
bio-signals. Each vest contains 18 sensors for EIT and respiratory sound recording
and the “master” unit sensor (see Figure 1.2 and Figure 1.3), which is responsible
for powering and coordination of sensors, wireless communication, and data concen-
tration and storage.
Figure 1.2: (Top images) - WELMO vests in different perspectives. (Bottom images, from
left to right) - WELMO master sensor and sensors’ harness; Sensor detail; Master sensor
detail.

6 CHAPTER 1. INTRODUCTION
Figure 1.3: (Left image) - WELMO tablet application paired with the master sensor.
(Right image) - WELMO vest use-case example in clinical setting.
In terms of the overall architecture of the WELMO system, the wearable vest
works in conjunction with a tablet application and a cloud-based computer. The
vest continuously records the bio-signals, which are then uploaded to a tablet ap-
plication via wireless connectivity, orchestrated by the “master” sensor. Then, the
tablet application acts as an interface for managing the data and transmitting it
to a cloud server for further processing. The tablet application also displays the
recorded signals in real-time in a graphical interface and provides sensor contact
information, which is crucial to ensure good quality signals. Once in the cloud,
algorithms analyze the collected signals, extracting key insights such as airflow pat-
terns, the presence of abnormal respiratory sounds such as wheezes or crackles, and
extraction ventilation-related measures from EIT, among other aspects. Clinicians
can then access these processed insights through a web application, where they
can monitor their patients’ collected data, review trends over time, and analyze
extracted insights. This integrated system enables continuous, remote monitoring,
allowing timely interventions and more efficient disease management, particularly in
chronic respiratory conditions. The work conducted during this thesis was on the
line of algorithm development for the processing of data collected with the wearable
vest.
1.4 Scientific Outcomes
During this thesis, several contributions to the field of automated respiratory func-
tion analysis were made. These include publishing main-authored and co-authored
articles in international peer-reviewed journals (3 articles as first-author in Q1 jour-
nals) and conferences (2 articles as first author), book chapters (1 article as first
author), participating in international conferences, scientific challenges, and special
sessions, and co-supervising several master’s degree theses. These are enumerated
in the following sections (chronologically ordered).

1.5 Thesis Outline/Structure
The current thesis was structured as a compilation of articles. The first-authored
publications that resulted from this thesis were compiled into the current document
in the same template as they were published in the respective journals/conferences.
Regarding the structural organization of the document:
• Chapter 2 introduces several background concepts related to the anatomy and
physiology of the human respiratory system and respiratory diseases. The chapter
also provides an overview of clinical standard methods that are used for respi-
ratory function assessment, as well as for the diagnosis of respiratory diseases.
Finally, the chapter provides a broad overview and some underlying concepts re-
lated to the two main bio-signals that were used during the current thesis, namely
respiratory sound and EIT.
• Chapter 3 provides an overview of the main thematic trends in the literature
concerning the automatic analysis of respiratory sounds and EIT.
• Chapter 4 is a compilation of the articles published during the thesis. The articles
were aggregated into multiple sections based on major thematics.
• Chapter 5 concludes the thesis by summarizing the essential contributions and
discussing potential future research directions.
