14 CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND CONCEPTS
2.1 Human Respiratory System
The respiratory system is vital for human beings as it facilitates the exchange of
oxygen and carbon dioxide between the body and the environment, namely the oxy-
genation of and elimination of carbon dioxide from the mixed venous blood. Such
exchange is fundamental for maintaining life as it allows the trillions of cells in
the human body to carry out crucial functions, such as reproduction, growth, and
repair/rebuild [8]. Generically, four major processes collectively facilitate these pro-
cesses: pulmonary ventilation, external respiration, transport of respiratory gases,
and internal respiration. In the following subsections, some general fundamentals
of the anatomy and physiology of the respiratory system are presented, as well as
some of the most common respiratory diseases. The primary aim of the following is
to summarize the essential features of respiration by introducing the anatomy and
physiology of the respiratory system.
2.1.1 Anatomy of the Respiratory System
The respiratory system is the network of organs and tissue involved in the respiration
process that work together with the primary objective of tissue oxygenation [9].
These form a continuous system of cavities or passages called the respiratory tract,
through which air enters and exits the body.
In generic terms, the system can be organized into regions based on anatomy
or function. Anatomically, the respiratory system can be divided into the upper
and lower respiratory tract. The upper respiratory tract includes the organs located
outside of the chest cavity (thorax) area (i.e., nose, pharynx, larynx). In contrast,
the lower respiratory tract includes the organs located almost entirely within it (i.e.,
trachea, bronchi, bronchiole, alveolar duct, alveoli) [10]. Functionally, there is the
conducting zone (from the nose to the bronchioles), which consists of the respiratory
organs forming a path to inhaled air into the deep lung region. The respiratory zone
(from the alveolar duct to the alveoli) consists of the alveoli and the tiny passageways
that open into them where the gas exchange occurs [10]. In Figure 2.1, the different
regions of the respiratory system are represented, as well as the main structures
within each region.
Upper Respiratory Tract
The upper respiratory tract (see Figure 2.1) is made up of the nose (nasal cavity),
the mouth (oral cavity), the pharynx, and the larynx. Some of its functions include
enabling smell and speech and ensuring that air entering the lower respiratory tract
is warm, damp, clean, and moist [11]. Below, the structures composing the upper
respiratory tract are described.

2.1. HUMAN RESPIRATORY SYSTEM 15
20 2 The Human Respiratory System
Fig. 2.1 Schematic of the respiratory system displayed by the upper and lower respiratory tract
region
place. Anatomically, the respiratory system can be divided into the upper and lower
respiratory tract. The upper respiratory tract includes the organs located outside of the
chest cavity (thorax) area (i.e. nose, pharynx, larynx), whereas the lower respiratory
tract includes the organs located almost entirely within it (i.e. trachea, bronchi,
bronchiole, alveolar duct, alveoli). In the next section, the individual respiratory
organs are discussed.
2.2 Nose and Nasal Cavity
2.2.1 Anatomy of the Nose and Nasal Cavity
The human nose differs in its anatomy and morphology between different racial and
ethnic groups. Therefore, the following anatomical description is a generalisation
and inter-racial variations exist. For completeness, structures that have synonyms
are followed by their alternate names in parentheses and italicised. Structurally the
nose can be divided into the external portion which is in fact termed as the nose and
the internal portions being the nasal cavities (nasal fossae; cavum nasi). The nose is
the only visible part of the respiratory system, protruding from the face, and lying
Figure 2.1: Schematic representation of the respiratory system and its main structures,
divided by upper and lower tract (adapted from [10]).
Nasal cavity: The nasal cavity is the space inside the nose. It lies above the bone
that forms the roof of the mouth and curves down at the back to join the throat. It
is divided into two sections called nasal passages. Air moves through these passages
during breathing. The nasal passages filter and warm the air, and moisten it before
it enters the lungs. They also contain special cells that are involved in the sense of
smell.
Oral cavity: The oral cavity, or mouth, is an oval-shaped opening that sits just
below the nose. It starts at the lips and ends towards the tonsils, at the end of
the throat, and is part of the respiratory and digestive systems. Similarly to the
nose, one of the main functions of the oral cavity within the respiratory system is
air intake. Given the larger size of the cavity, it can bring in more air in a lower
temporal interval. However, unlike the nasal cavity, it does not possess any filtration
system to clear and warm the incoming air, which might dry out the gums and cause
issues like bad breath and dry mouth.
Pharynx: The pharynx (throat) is a hollow, tube-like muscular structure that
connects the posterior nasal and oral cavities to the larynx and esophagus [10].
Structurally, the pharynx can be divided into three anatomical parts according to its
location, which are the nasopharynx (posterior to the nasal chambers), the orophar-
ynx (posterior to the mouth), and the laryngopharynx (posterior to the pharynx).
The pharynx acts as a passageway for air to enter the larynx and lungs and for food
and liquid to enter the esophagus, making it a common passage that is part of both
the respiratory and digestive systems. It also plays an important role in speech.

16 CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND CONCEPTS
Larynx: The larynx is a mobile structure that connects the pharynx and the tra-
chea. It is also commonly known as the “voice box”, which houses the vocal folds re-
sponsible for sound production (phonation). The larynx consists of cartilage, joints,
ligaments, muscles, and mucosa, all of which interact to permit its essential func-
tions. Some of these cartilages allow the separation of the vocal cords to facilitate
the breathing process. In contrast, others enable the movement of the vocal cords to
produce the various types of sounds emitted by humans. Besides its essential role in
speech production, the larynx also acts as a protective buffer of the lower airways. It
prevents the aspiration of secretions or ingested food in the lower respiratory tract,
thus preventing choking.
Lower Respiratory Tract
The lower respiratory tract refers to the parts of the respiratory system below the
cricoid cartilage and vocal cords, including the inferior part of the larynx, tracheo-
bronchial tree (trachea, bronchi, bronchioles, and alveoli), and lungs (see Figure 2.1).
Below, the structures composing the lower respiratory tract are described.
Trachea: The trachea is the longest extrapulmonary conducting airway in the
respiratory system. It is a hollow tube of cartilage, connective tissue, smooth muscle,
and a mucosal layer connecting the larynx to the principal bronchi and the lungs.
Its typical dimension range is between 11 to 14 cm [10]. This rigid tube is designed
for conducting gas, with rigid C-shaped hyaline cartilage rings that protect it from
frontal injury and also prevent collapse during the negative changes in intrathoracic
pressure that occur during respiration [12]. The trachea extends from the larynx
and bifurcates into divides into the main bronchi (primary bronchi) at the carina,
with the right bronchus wider, shorter, and more vertical than the left bronchus.
The passageway from the trachea bifurcates into main bronchi, which further divide
into lobar, then segmental bronchi, and continues this bifurcating process down to
the terminal bronchioles (which are the smallest airways without alveoli) is called
the conducting airways. In this area, gas exchange does not take place because no
alveoli are present.
Lungs: The lung is a conditionally renewing organ with very low homeostatic cell
turnover [13]. In the human body, there are two lungs. These are cone-shaped,
lightweight, elastic, and spongy organs, almost filling the thorax, and the ribs, ster-
num, and vertebrae protect them. The top of each lung, the apex, extends just above
the clavicle (collarbone), and the bases sit just above the diaphragm (muscle). The
lungs sit within the pleural cavity of the chest, separated by an area known as the
mediastinum, where the heart is located. Each lung is enclosed by a double-layered
membrane called the pleura. The pleural space is a thin, fluid-filled cavity between
the lungs and the chest wall’s inner surface. This potential space is lined by two

2.1. HUMAN RESPIRATORY SYSTEM 17
serous membrane layers: the visceral pleura, which covers the lung surfaces, and
the parietal pleura, which lines the chest wall’s interior surface. These membranes
secrete a small amount of pleural fluid, lubricating the pleural surfaces and reducing
friction during breathing [14]. Each lung is also divided into lobes separated from
one another by a tissue fissure. The right lung has three major lobes; the left lung
has two lobes, which is slightly smaller because of the asymmetrical heart place-
ment. Internally, each lobe further subdivides into hundreds of lobules. Each lobule
contains a bronchiole and affiliated branches, a thin wall, and clusters of alveoli.
Bronchi, bronchioles, and alveoli: The bronchi, bronchioles, and alveoli con-
stitute what is commonly designated as the “bronchial tree” . In the mediastinum,
at the level of the fifth thoracic vertebra, the trachea divides into the right and
left primary bronchi. The bronchi branch into smaller and smaller passageways
until they terminate in tiny air sacs called alveoli. The cartilage and mucous mem-
brane of the primary bronchi are similar to that in the trachea. As the branching
continues through the bronchial tree, the amount of hyaline cartilage in the walls
decreases until it is absent in the smallest bronchioles. The gas exchange begins in
the respiratory bronchioles, where capillaries are close enough to inspire air for ef-
fective gas diffusion [13]. The bronchioles continue to branch, giving rise to alveolar
ducts, which lead to alveolar sacs or clusters of microscopic alveoli. As the carti-
lage decreases, the amount of smooth muscle increases. The mucous membrane also
undergoes a transition from ciliated pseudostratified columnar epithelium to simple
cuboidal epithelium to simple squamous epithelium.
The alveolar ducts and alveoli consist primarily of simple squamous epithelium,
which permits rapid diffusion of oxygen and carbon dioxide [15]. The alveoli com-
prise two types of cells, namely type I and type II pneumatocytes. Type I pneu-
mocytes are thin, flat cells that form most of the alveolar epithelium. Type II
pneumocytes are cuboidal cells interspersed among type I pneumocytes [14]. Each
alveolus (singular of alveoli) is about 200 micrometers ( µm) in diameter. Further-
more, all the alveoli structures together form a very large surface area of about
130 square meters (m2) [16]. The two lungs can contain up to 700 million alveoli,
providing an enormous total surface area for gas exchange [17].
Respiratory muscles: In addition to the extensive network of passages and or-
gans bronchial tree, the muscles involved in breathing are also typically considered
part of the lower respiratory tract. The respiratory muscles fulfill a vital function,
maintaining correct ventilation at a systemic level. The primary respiratory mus-
cles are the diaphragm and external intercostals. Anatomically, the diaphragm is
shaped like a dome, with a convex surface directed to the abdomen and a concave
face directed to the chest cavity, separating the abdominal cavity from the thoracic
one [18]. In normal individuals, this muscle is responsible for 70% of the total respi-

18 CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND CONCEPTS
ratory function and 40% of the tidal volume at rest [18]. The diaphragm contraction
during the respiratory cycle changes the anatomical configuration of the chest and
moves its components. This ultimately allows air to flow passively into the airways
of the respiratory system because of the volume increase, which, subsequently, leads
to a drop in pressure within the lungs, allowing the gas exchanges to occur. While
the diaphragm and intercostal muscles play a vital role in the breathing process, by
manipulating the rib cage, their respiratory action is assisted and augmented by a
complex set of other auxiliary muscle groups (abdominals, sternocleidomastoid, sub-
costals, among others). Additionally, the muscles of the larynx, pharynx, and nasal
cavity adjust the resistance to the movement of gases through the upper respiratory
pathways during both inhalation and exhalation. [17].
2.1.2 Physiology and Function of the Respiratory System
According to current physiological definitions, respiration is the “interchange of gases
between an organism and the medium in which it lives” [19]. For humans, this gas
exchange consists of obtaining oxygen from the atmosphere and removing carbon
dioxide from the blood. Both compounds are of extreme importance for homeostatic
regulation in the human body. Oxygen is critical for proper metabolism on a cellular
level, while carbon dioxide is crucial for achieving adequate PH levels. At rest, an
average human breathes 12–15 times a minute [20]. Each breath containing 500
mL of air translates to 6–8 L of air that is inspired and expired every minute. On
average, 250 mL of oxygen enters the body per minute, and 200 mL of carbon dioxide
is excreted [20].
Several mechanisms exist to ensure a rigorous balance between supply and de-
mand. In response to a change in blood gases, the pulmonary system adapts by
adjusting breathing patterns to help meet the metabolic demand of the body [21,22].
The body uses multiple strategies to control the balance of these two compounds,
namely thought regulation and control of respiration, breathing, and gas exchanges.
2.1.2.1 Respiration Control and Regulation
Despite gas exchange taking place in the lungs, the respiratory system is controlled
by the central nervous system. While we have some voluntary control of breathing,
the entire process is regulated automatically and functions unconsciously.
The portions of the Central Nervous System (CNS) that control respiration are
located within the brain stem, specifically within the pons (apneustic center)and
the medulla (pneumotaxic center). These components are responsible for the nerve
impulses transmitted via the phrenic and other motor nerves to the diaphragm and
intercostal muscles, controlling our basic breathing rhythm. The central chemore-
ceptors are also located in the brainstem. These specialized cells signal the body to
adjust ventilation based indirectly on arterial CO2 (PaCO2) levels. This accounts for

2.1. HUMAN RESPIRATORY SYSTEM 19
the primary respiratory drive of the human body. The peripheral chemoreceptors,
which are located outside of the brainstem in the carotid and aortic arteries, serve
as the body’s backup respiratory drive by responding to low levels of oxygen [23].
Respiration is regulated by two distinct neural mechanisms: one for voluntary
control and the other for automatic control. The voluntary system in the cerebral
cortex sends impulses to the respiratory motor neurons via the corticospinal tracts.
The automatic system, driven by pacemaker cells in the medulla, activates motor
neurons in the cervical and thoracic spinal cord, which innervate the inspiratory
muscles. Specifically, the cervical spinal cord motor neurons activate the diaphragm
through the phrenic nerves, while those in the thoracic spinal cord stimulate the
external intercostal muscles. These impulses also extend to the internal intercostal
muscles and other expiratory muscles. The main components of the respiratory
control pattern generator responsible for automatic respiration are located in the
medulla. Rhythmic respiration is initiated by a small group of synaptically coupled
pacemaker cells in the pre-B¨ otzinger complex. These neurons discharge rhythmi-
cally, producing rhythmic discharges in phrenic motor neurons that are abolished
by sections between the pre-B¨ otzinger complex and these motor neurons [20].
Respiration can be voluntarily commanded for some periods of time, but even-
tually, the voluntary control is overridden. The breaking point is the point at which
breathing can no longer be voluntarily inhibited. Breaking is due to the rise in
arterial PCO2 and the fall in PO 2.
Besides the previously mentioned mechanisms, chemical control can also regulate
the breathing process. A rise in the PCO 2 or H+ concentration of arterial blood or
a drop in its PO 2 increases the level of respiratory neuron activity in the medulla,
and changes in the opposite direction have a slight inhibitory effect. The effects of
variations in blood chemistry on ventilation are mediated via respiratory chemore-
ceptors, the carotid and aortic bodies, and collections of cells in the medulla and
elsewhere that are sensitive to changes in the chemistry of the blood. They initiate
impulses that stimulate the respiratory center [20].
2.1.2.2 Mechanism of Breathing
The most obvious component of respiration is breathing, which is a cyclical process
involving two movements, namely inspiration (inhalation) and expiration (exhala-
tion). Breathing is also referred to as pulmonary ventilation.
In broader terms, ventilation, or breathing, is air movement through the con-
ducting passages between the atmosphere and the lungs. The air moves through the
passages because of pressure gradients produced by the contraction of the diaphragm
and thoracic muscles. When there is a decrease in air pressure, the alveolar spaces
fall, and the air enters the lungs (inspiration). As the pressure of the alveoli within
exceeds the atmospheric pressure, the air is blown from the lungs (expiration) [24].

20 CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND CONCEPTS
The airflow rate is proportional to the magnitude of the pressure difference. While
inspiration is an active process that involves the expansion of the chest cavity and
the inhalation of air, expiration is a passive process in which the reduction of the
chest cavity size leads to the expulsion of air from the lungs to the environment.
Figure 2.2 illustrate the mechanism associated with inspiration and expiration.
Figure 2.2: Schematic representation of the breathing mechanism (adapted from [11]).
Inspiration starts with the thorax expansion, lowering the pressure within the
lungs compared to the atmospheric pressure. Then, air will naturally move into
the airways until the pressure difference no longer exists. The contraction of the
external intercostal muscles and the diaphragm causes this expansion.
On the other hand, during expiration, the diaphragm and abdomen muscles
relax, decreasing the volume of the chest cavity and compressing the lungs. Subse-
quently, the compression of the lungs increases the internal pressure, causing the air
in the lungs to be passively pushed out through the nose.
2.1.2.3 Mechanism of Respiration (Gas Exchanges)
While breathing, or pulmonary ventilation, is a mechanical process and involves the
movement of air inwards and outwards of the respiratory tract, respiration is a phys-
iologic process that involves the exchange of gases between the air and circulating
blood in the alveoli (external respiration) and the cells (internal respiration). The
process of respiration is controlled by complex hormonal and neural signaling, which
detects concentrations of oxygen and CO2 and acts to alter the amount of blood flow
through the capillaries or the size of the smooth muscle bronchioles that connect the
alveoli to the rest of the airway.

2.1. HUMAN RESPIRATORY SYSTEM 21
Respiratory gases move between the environment and respiring tissues through
two primary mechanisms: convection and diffusion. Convection facilitates air move-
ment from the environment into the lungs and blood transport between the lungs
and tissues. On the other hand, diffusion occurs across tissue barriers such as mem-
branes and is the main mode of gas transport between air and blood in the lungs and
between blood and respiring tissues in the body. This process is driven by differences
in the partial pressure of gases, with gases moving from areas of high pressure (high
concentration) to low pressure (low concentration). During inspiration, the oxygen
concentration is high in the alveoli and low in the blood capillaries, causing oxy-
gen to move from the alveoli to the capillaries. Conversely, carbon dioxide follows
an inverse gradient, diffusing from the blood to the alveoli to be expelled during
expiration. Figure 2.3 illustrates the diffusion process.
Figure 2.3: Schematic representation of gas exchange between alveolar spaces and capil-
laries (image from [25]).
Once the blood has been oxygenated in the lungs, it is transported to the tissue
capillaries, where oxygen diffuses into the cells. Conversely, carbon dioxide diffuses
from the body’s cells into the blood. This gas exchange between the cells and red
blood cells in the capillaries mirrors the process occurring in the alveoli. Known
as internal respiration, this process involves no exchange of gases with the external
environment. As blood flows through the capillaries, oxygen and carbon dioxide
follow their pressure gradients, continuously diffusing between blood and tissue.
Since oxygen and carbon dioxide are too poorly soluble in blood to be adequately
transported in solution, specialized systems for each gas have evolved to increase the

22 CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND CONCEPTS
quantities of those gases that can be transported in blood. These systems are present
mainly in the red blood cells, which comprise 40 to 50 percent of the blood volume
in most mammals. Most oxygen is bound to hemoglobin, a protein within red cells,
for transport in the bloodstream. Conversely, carbon dioxide is either attached to
hemoglobin or dissolved in blood plasma as part of the bicarbonate buffer system.
These storage systems allow carbon dioxide to be exhaled by the lungs (hemoglobin
detachment) or excreted by the kidneys (dissolved in plasma as part of the buffer
system).
2.1.3 Respiratory Diseases
Respiratory diseases are some of the most common medical conditions worldwide,
affecting millions of people. Such diseases may affect any of the structures and
organs that have to do with breathing, including the nasal cavities, the pharynx
(or throat), the larynx, the trachea (or windpipe), the bronchi and bronchioles, the
tissues of the lungs, and the respiratory muscles of the chest cage. These may be
caused by infection, smoking tobacco, or breathing in secondhand tobacco smoke,
radon, asbestos, or other forms of air pollution.
One of the great challenges related to respiratory diseases is that several of them
are not curable; they are chronic diseases. Such diseases have a really high impact
on the life of the patients as they might impair several body functions (e.g., breath-
ing, muscle strength, exercise tolerance) and structures (e.g., respiratory system,
muscles), leading to restrictions on regular daily life activities (e.g., exercising, en-
gaging in leisure and recreation activities) [26]. Therefore, for those suffering from
such diseases, it is of utmost importance to be diagnosed early in order to man-
age such diseases and their complications better. This section provides a generic
overview of some of the most common respiratory diseases, such as Chronic obstruc-
tive pulmonary disease (COPD), asthma, Interstitial Lung Disease (ILD), bronchi-
olitis, bronchiectasis, pneumonia, and pleural effusion, among others.
Respiratory Diseases Affecting the Airways
These diseases affect the tubes (airways) that carry oxygen and other gases into
and out of the lungs. They usually cause a narrowing or blockage of the airways.
The two most common chronic respiratory diseases are asthma and COPD. Other
diseases related to airway dysfunction are bronchiolitis and bronchiectasis.
Asthma: Asthma is a chronic lung disease affecting both children and adults and
is the most common chronic disease among children. In 2019, asthma affected an
estimated 262 million people and caused 455000 deaths [27]. It is caused by inflam-
mation and muscle tightening around the airways, making breathing harder.

2.1. HUMAN RESPIRATORY SYSTEM 23
This disease is primarily characterized by wheezing, shortness of breath, chest
tightness, and/or cough, along with variable limitation of the respiratory airflow.
These symptoms can be mild, intermittent, or severe and can come and go over
time. Usually, when severe, they can be continuous over extended periods of time.
Allergies, pollution, exercise, and cold temperatures can trigger asthma symptoms
[28].
Because asthma is a clinical syndrome, there is no gold standard for its diagnosis
[28]. Diagnosis is based on physical exam, symptoms, and medical history. However,
several tests to examine pulmonary function can be used, such as spirometry, peak
flow, chest X-ray, and allergy tests, among others.
Asthma cannot be cured, but several treatments are available. The most com-
mon treatment is using an inhaler, which delivers medication directly to the lungs.
Inhalers can help control the disease and enable people with asthma to enjoy a nor-
mal, active life. There are two main types of inhalers: bronchodilators, which open
the air passages and relieve symptoms, and steroids, which reduce inflammation in
the air passages, improving asthma symptoms and reducing the risk of severe asthma
attacks and death.
COPD: COPD is an umbrella term encompassing several types of chronic respi-
ratory diseases where the airways in the lungs become swollen and partially blocked.
Such conditions typically worsen over time and cannot be cured, only treated and
managed to mitigate symptoms [28]. According to the World Health Organization,
in 2019, COPD was the third leading cause of death worldwide, causing 3.23 million
deaths [29].
The most common symptoms of COPD are difficulty breathing, chronic cough
with increased mucus production (sometimes with phlegm or sputum), respiratory
infections, and wheezing. They develop from mid-life onwards. As COPD progresses,
patients find it more challenging to carry out their normal daily activities, often due
to breathlessness. There may also be a considerable financial burden due to the
limitation of workplace and home productivity and the costs of medical treatment.
COPD consists of two major breathing diseases: emphysema and chronic bron-
chitis [28]. Emphysema damages the tiny alveoli (air sacs) at the end of the airways
in the lungs. They lose elasticity and can become damaged. Chronic bronchitis
affects the lining of the airways, which become constantly irritated and inflamed. It
is usually referred to as a chronic cough, which produces phlegm from inflammation
in the airways.
A diagnosis of COPD is based on signs and symptoms, history of exposure to
lung irritants (such as smoking), and family history. There is no cure for COPD,
and the damaged lung tissue cannot repair itself. Some forms of treatment in-
clude inhalers with bronchodilator medication, oxygen therapy, expectorants, and
pulmonary rehabilitation, among others.

24 CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND CONCEPTS
Bronchioloitis: Bronchiolitis is an acute inflammatory injury of the smaller air-
ways (bronchioles) usually caused by a viral infection (most commonly respiratory
syncytial virus). When these airways become infected or damaged, they can become
inflamed or congested. This inflammation/congestion reduces, or blocks, oxygen flow
to the alveoli, where the gas exchange process should occur normally. This condi-
tion may occur in persons of any age, but severe symptoms are usually evident only
in young infants. Bronchiolitis typically affects children younger than 24 months
and is most common among infants 2 to 6 months. Each year, 150 million children
worldwide are diagnosed. The main symptoms of this condition include shortness
of breath and fatigue, audible wheezing sounds during breathing, crackling sounds,
rapid breathing, mild fever, cough, nasal congestion, and mucus.
Bronchiectasis: Bronchiectasis is a long-term condition characterized by persis-
tent and lifelong widening of the lungs’ airways. This widening leads to a build-up
of excess mucus that can make the lungs more vulnerable to infection. The widening
of the airways also weakens the mucociliary transport mechanism, contributing to
bacterial invasion and mucus pooling throughout the bronchial tree.
The symptoms of bronchiectasis can take months or even years to manifest.
Common symptoms include a persistent daily cough, coughing up blood, abnormal
chest sounds or wheezing, shortness of breath, chest pain, expelling large amounts
of thick mucus daily, constant fatigue, and frequent respiratory infections. Although
there is no cure for bronchiectasis, it is manageable with effective treatment, allowing
individuals to maintain a normal life. Prompt treatment of exacerbations is crucial
to ensure proper oxygen flow to the rest of the body and to prevent further airway
damage.
Respiratory Diseases Affecting the Air Sacs (Alveoli)
Airways branch into tiny tubes (bronchioles) that end in clusters of air sacs called
alveoli. Lung diseases that affect the air sacs include:
Pneumonia: Pneumonia may be defined as an infection of the lung character-
istically involving the alveolar space, leading to the accumulation of pus or other
fluids [30]. The Global Burden of Disease Study 2015 found Lower Respiratory Tract
Infections (LRTI) were the leading infectious cause of death and the fifth leading
cause of death overall. Pneumococcal pneumonia caused 55% of LRTI deaths in all
ages (1.5 million deaths) [30].
Pneumonia can be caused by various microorganisms, including bacteria, viruses,
fungi, and even chemical irritants. It can result in a range of symptoms, from mild
to severe, namely, fever, cough, difficulty breathing, chest pain, and fatigue, and
it may lead to significant health complications if not properly treated. Diagnosis

2.1. HUMAN RESPIRATORY SYSTEM 25
is suggested based on medical history and lung examination findings. It should be
confirmed by chest radiography or ultrasonography [28].
Pulmonary edema: Pulmonary edema is a medical condition characterized by
the accumulation of excessive extravascular fluid in the air sacs (alveoli) and the
interstitium of the lungs [31]. The symptoms of pulmonary edema can vary depend-
ing on its severity and the underlying cause. However, they often include severe
shortness of breath, especially when lying down, a persistent cough, often producing
pink or frothy sputum, rapid, shallow breathing, anxiety or restlessness, wheezing
or crackling sounds in the lungs, cyanosis (bluish discoloration of the skin and lips)
due to a lack of oxygen [17].
Pulmonary edema is often caused by congestive heart failure. When the heart
cannot pump efficiently, blood can back up into the veins that take blood through
the lungs. As the pressure in these blood vessels increases, fluid is pushed into the air
spaces (alveoli) in the lungs. This fluid reduces normal oxygen movement through
the lungs. These two factors combine to cause shortness of breath.
The other type of pulmonary edema, non-cardiogenic pulmonary edema, is not
exclusively related to heart-related issues. It can be caused by factors like acute
respiratory distress syndrome, pneumonia, lung infections, exposure to toxic gases
or chemicals, high-altitude pulmonary edema (HAPE), or severe kidney disease.
Respiratory Diseases Affecting the Interstitium
The interstitium is the thin, delicate lining between the alveoli. It consists of a
network of tiny blood vessels, connective tissue, and air spaces, and its primary
function is to support the alveoli and facilitate the exchange of oxygen and carbon
dioxide between the lungs and the bloodstream. These diseases affect the structure
of the lung tissue, namely the interstitium. Scarring or inflammation of the tissue
makes the lungs unable to expand fully (restrictive lung disease). It also disrupts
the exchange of gases in the lungs.
ILD: ILD describes a large group of disorders, most of which cause progressive
scarring (fibrosis) and inflammation of lung tissue. The scarring associated with
interstitial lung disease eventually affects the ability to breathe and get enough
oxygen into the bloodstream. Idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis, sarcoidosis, alveolitis,
and autoimmune diseases are some of the most frequent ILD.
The most common symptom of all ILDs is shortness of breath, often accompanied
by a dry cough, chest discomfort, fatigue, and occasionally weight loss. In most cases,
lung damage has already been done by the time the symptoms appear.

26 CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND CONCEPTS
Respiratory Diseases Affecting the Blood Vessels
These diseases affect the blood vessels in the lungs. They are caused by clotting,
scarring, or inflammation of the blood vessels and affect the ability of the lungs to
take up oxygen and release carbon dioxide. These diseases may also affect heart
function.
Pulmonary embolism: A pulmonary embolism is a blood clot that blocks and
stops blood flow to an artery in the lung. In most cases, the blood clot starts in a
deep vein in the leg and travels to the lung. The clot sticks in a pulmonary artery,
often causing shortness of breath and low blood oxygen levels. Pulmonary embolism
can be life-threatening.
Pulmonary hypertension: Pulmonary hypertension is high blood pressure in
the blood vessels that supply the lungs (pulmonary arteries). The walls of the
pulmonary arteries become thick and stiff and cannot expand as well to allow blood
passage. The reduced blood flow makes it harder for the right side of the heart to
pump blood through the arteries. It can lead to shortness of breath and chest pain.
Respiratory Diseases Affecting the Pleura
The pleura is a thin layer of tissue that covers the lungs and lines the interior wall
of the chest cavity. It protects and cushions the lungs. This tissue secretes a small
amount of fluid that acts as a lubricant, allowing the lungs to move smoothly in the
chest cavity while breathing. Lung diseases of the pleura include:
Pleural effusion: Pleural effusion is the buildup of excess fluid between the layers
of the pleura outside the lungs. There are two types of pleural effusion: transudative
pleural effusion, caused by fluid leaking into the pleural space due to increased
pressure in the blood vessels or a low blood protein count, and exudative pleural
effusion, caused by blocked blood vessels or lymph vessels, inflammation, infection,
lung injury, or tumors. Pleural effusion might also result from pneumonia or heart
failure.
Pneumothorax: A pneumothorax is a partial or complete lung collapse due to
air between the two layers of the pleura. The two main types of pneumothorax are
spontaneous and traumatic. Spontaneous pneumothorax is a collapsed lung that
happens without an injury. Conversely, traumatic pneumothorax is the result of
an injury from medical procedures. Pneumothorax can be diagnosed using lung
ultrasound or chest X-ray.

2.2. RESPIRATORY DISEASE ASSESSMENT 27
Respiratory Diseases Affection the Chest Wall
The chest wall also plays an important role in breathing. All the musculature con-
nected to the ribs within the chest wall plays a pivotal role in mechanical ventilation,
helping the chest to expand or contract. Diseases that affect the chest wall include:
Obesity hypoventilation syndrome: Extra weight on the chest and belly region
can make it difficult for the chest to expand, which can cause serious breathing
problems.
Neuromuscular disorders: Due to problems in the conduction of electrical sig-
nals, the nerves that control the respiratory muscles do not work the way they
should, disrupting their activation in mechanical ventilation. Amyotrophic lateral
sclerosis and myasthenia gravis are two examples of neuromuscular lung disease.
2.2 Respiratory Disease Assessment
Assessing respiratory diseases is a critical component of medical practice, involving a
comprehensive evaluation of the respiratory system to diagnose and manage various
pulmonary conditions. Besides physical observation and patient history, respiratory
medicine also relies on several diagnostic techniques, namely, pulmonary function
tests, imaging studies, and laboratory tests, each providing essential information
about the structure and function of the respiratory tract. However, despite today’s
technological developments, a proper history and a sound physical examination have
no substitute as the initial step in making a correct diagnosis and, consequently,
providing the appropriate treatment [32]. Respiratory diseases significantly impact
the quality of life and healthcare systems worldwide; thus, meticulous assessment
not only facilitates early diagnosis and treatment but also informs prognosis and
guides long-term management strategies, aiming to improve patient outcomes and
reduce morbidity and mortality.
Currently, auscultation, spirometry (or other respiratory function tests), and
medical imaging are some of the most commonly used techniques in clinical practice
when assessing respiratory function and diagnosing respiratory diseases. Together,
these diagnostic tools enable healthcare providers to accurately diagnose respira-
tory conditions, assess disease severity, and monitor the effectiveness of treatments.
Despite being largely used for many years by physicians, these techniques suffer
from significant drawbacks that limit their effectiveness, especially in the context of
wearable and continuous monitoring.

28 CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND CONCEPTS
2.2.1 Respiratory Auscultation
Chest auscultation has long been considered a useful and invaluable part of the phys-
ical examination, going back to the time of Hippocrates [33]. Notwithstanding, was
not until the nineteenth century, in 1816, that auscultation became a widespread
examination method, with the development of the stethoscope by the French med-
ical doctor Ren´ e La¨ ennec, who sought a more effective way to listen to the chest
sounds of his patients without direct contact (see Figure 2.4) [34]. The French
also invented the name stethoscope, resulting from the concatenation of two Greek
words: “stethos” (chest) and “skopein” (to observe). From that moment, ausculta-
tion quickly became popular worldwide. Twenty-five years after La¨ ennec invented
the stethoscope, George P. Camman developed a design that included a headset for
each ear [34]. This design would be used for more than 100 years with very few
modifications.
Figure 2.4: Early stethoscope developed by La¨ ennec (image from [35]).
Although the stethoscope emerged over 200 years ago, it remains, to this day,
an essential instrument in medical practice, symbolizing the physician’s role and
providing critical insights into the respiratory system, as well as other systems (e.g.,
cardiovascular and digestive). Lung auscultation, facilitated by the stethoscope,
continues to be a fundamental skill for healthcare professionals, allowing them to
detect and assess a wide range of respiratory diseases. In fact, the physician’s
ability to distinguish between normal and abnormal respiratory sounds (adventitious
respiratory sounds) remains essential in clinical practice for an efficient diagnosis.

2.2. RESPIRATORY DISEASE ASSESSMENT 29
Auscultation Principles
Respiratory sounds, also known as lung sounds or breath sounds, are specific sounds
generated by air movement through the respiratory system. The auscultation process
involves carefully listening to these sounds transmitted through the stethoscope. The
primary purpose of the stethoscope is to amplify these sounds, allowing for detailed
analysis.
Detecting abnormal sounds, such as Adventitious Respiratory Sounds (ARS),
during auscultation is crucial for the identification of several respiratory diseases.
The airflow sounds produced during mechanical ventilation differ when the airways
are obstructed, narrowed, or filled with liquid and mucus, providing valuable insights
for clinicians when auscultating their patients. In general, clinicians examine the
main areas of the bronchial tree in the anterior and posterior parts of the body.
Below are summarized some of the main considerations that should be taken into
account when auscultating respiratory sounds [36]:
• Auscultation should be done in a quiet room, preferably sitting (background noise
can interfere with listening to the sounds of interest; therefore, the patient should
remain silent).
• If the patient cannot assume a sitting posture, roll the patient from one side to
the other to examine the back. Also, the patient should take anywhere between
1 to 5 respiratory cycles.
• The examination room should be warm to ensure the patient’s comfort and to
prevent shivering that can be added as interfering noise; it should also be well-lit
to detect the positions that need to be auscultated accurately.
• Before placing the stethoscope into take with the patient’s body, it should be
warmed up by rubbing its chest piece in the hands/clothes before placing it on
the naked body. Auscultation should never be done through the clothing.
• During the examination, the patient should take deep breaths through the open
mouth. This allows for an increase in the intensity of the respiratory sounds of
interest, facilitating their detection.
• Three main thoracic areas should be considered for auscultation areas: the pos-
terior thoracic surface, the anterior thoracic surface, and the lateral thoracic
surface.
• Auscultation should be performed systematically and symmetrically between the
left and right sides to detect any asymmetry between the sounds in both lungs. A
“stepladder” approach is commonly used to auscultate different regions following
a zig-zag pattern.
• During auscultation, the listener should look for the quality of the breath sounds,
the intensity of the breath sounds, and the presence of ARS. To identify ARS is

30 CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND CONCEPTS
essential to know their characteristics such as frequency, pitch, intensity, sonority,
timbre, and duration, among others.
Auscultation Devices (Stethoscopes/Microphones)
Listening to respiratory sounds (using a stethoscope or a microphone) involves sev-
eral physical phenomena: vibrations of the chest wall are converted into pressure
variations of the air in the stethoscope, and those pressure variations are then trans-
mitted to the diaphragm of the ear [37]. Therefore, the basic principle in auscultation
with a stethoscope is the transfer of vibrations of the chest wall to the air pressure
variations that travel to the diaphragm of the ear throughout the stethoscope [38].
Two major types of auscultation devices exist: conventional stethoscopes and elec-
tronic stethoscopes/microphones. Such devices can be further divided based on the
approach used to capture the respiratory sound: the “kinematic” approach, which
involves the direct recording of chest wall movement (“contact sensor”), and the
“acoustic” approach, which involves the recording of the movement of a diaphragm
exposed to the pressure wave induced by the chest wall movement (“air-coupled
sensor”). The chest wall movements are so weak that a free-field recording is not
possible; therefore, it is essential to couple the diaphragm acoustically with the chest
wall through a closed-air cavity [37, 38]. Notwithstanding whatever the approach,
kinematic or acoustic, the captured vibrations have to be converted into electrical
signals. To achieve that, three major principles of transduction are applied: 1)
electromagnetic induction, a small movable induction coil’s movement in the mag-
netic field induces a varying current in the coil through electromagnetic induction;
2) condenser principle: changing the distance between the two plates of a charged
capacitor induces a voltage fluctuation; 3) piezoelectric effect: if a crystal (rod, foil)
is bent, an electric charge on the surface is induced [39].
Conventional stethoscopes (or acoustic stethoscopes) are designed to acquire
sounds via chestpieces, which have different shapes optimized for cardiac and res-
piratory auscultation purposes, and transmit the sounds via tubes to the listener’s
ears [40]. A typical acoustic stethoscope consists of a chestpiece, flexible tubing, and
a headset with earpieces and a binaural spring [41]. The term acoustic stethoscope
is commonly used to refer to traditional air-coupled stethoscopes without electrical
transducers. The chestpiece is held against the patient’s skin by a clinician, upon
which vibrations originating from internal biological processes, such as heart beating
or air passing through the airways, induce pressure perturbations in the air cavity.
The flexible tubing forms a waveguide to transmit these pressure perturbations to
the two earpieces. At the same time, a “binaural spring” ensures that the earpieces
are securely held in the clinician’s ears [41].
Conventional acoustic stethoscopes do not allow for recording or streaming these
audio signals; thus, demanding a second opinion on the sounds acquired during a

2.2. RESPIRATORY DISEASE ASSESSMENT 31
visit is impossible. With emerging sensor technologies and the trend toward dig-
italization in health care, electronic stethoscopes have been introduced to address
this need. While the design of acoustic stethoscopes is severely constricted by the
fact that one end of the device needs to be directly coupled to the clinician’s ears,
electronic stethoscopes overcome this constraint by converting the vibrations of the
chest into electrical signals. This means that there no longer needs to be a fixed dis-
tance between the chestpiece and the ears of the clinician. Moreover, for electronic
stethoscopes, there is no requirement for the vibrations of the human body to be
converted into sound waves in air, vastly increasing the design space compared to
acoustic stethoscopes and allowing for a wide range of transducer technologies to be
employed [41]. Thus, not only have electronic stethoscopes enabled the recording of
the auscultation data and storage, but they also allowed the integration of acoustic
stethoscopes in the wearable format, creating an opportunity to analyze and process
the acquired sound signals for supporting clinicians’ diagnosis [40].
Air microphones are commonly used in wearable stethoscope developments. As
the name indicates, sound waves propagate in the air, and instead of directly reach-
ing the listener’s ear, as in the case of conventional stethoscopes, they are captured
by an air-coupled microphone. While certain applications employ micromachined ca-
pacitive or piezoelectric microphones to lower costs, electret condenser microphones
provide a higher SNR (up to 80 dB) [40]. An imminent drawback of air microphones
is their inherent susceptibility to capture ambient noise. Unless a very rigid wall
or multilayer sound absorption solutions are incorporated, ambient noise penetrates
through the sidewalls into the air chamber, significantly decreasing signal quality.
While air microphones necessitate a diaphragm, contact microphones can di-
rectly be interfaced with the skin. More precisely, sound waves can be coupled to
the sensor via a solid or liquid medium rather than air [40]. If appropriately de-
signed, contact microphones can provide better isolation from ambient noise as their
operation principle requires matching specific acoustic impedances of the transducer
element and the source. When compared to air microphones, contact microphones
also allow much higher impedance matching, which can only be achieved with un-
practical dimensions for air microphones. Consequently, the sound waves reach the
sensors with minimum attenuation, improving the signal-to-noise ratio [40].
Advantages and Limitations of Auscultation
Respiratory auscultation offers several advantages as a diagnostic tool, making it an
essential practice in clinical medicine. Auscultation is a non-invasive, inexpensive,
safe, quick, and easy-to-perform diagnostic technique physicians use to diagnose
multiple pulmonary diseases [36]. To date, auscultation remains one of the easiest
and quickest ways to provide relevant information regarding the respiratory sys-
tem, requiring minimal collaboration from patients [33]. Through auscultation, it is

32 CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND CONCEPTS
possible to extract relevant information on the physiology context of the lungs and
upper airways and analyze their acoustic patterns. Consequently, auscultation facil-
itates the assessment of the physiology and pathophysiology of several respiratory
disorders [42].
Conventional auscultation also has some drawbacks that limit its use in the clin-
ical context and academic research: 1) it cannot be performed frequently and, thus,
cannot provide continuous monitoring; 2) it needs to be performed by an expert, es-
pecially when trying to detect and determine the presence of abnormal sounds [43];
3) its inherent inter-listener variability [44]; 4) and the limits of human audition [45].
These limitations and drawbacks hinder the effectiveness of conventional ausculta-
tion as a means to monitor and manage respiratory conditions. It also means it can
be difficult for a patient to be auscultated away from clinical facilities.
2.2.2 Pulmonary Function Tests
Pulmonary Function Tests (PFTs) are complementary diagnostic tests for the res-
piratory system that are essential for assessing, diagnosing, and monitoring a wide
range of pulmonary conditions. These tests provide critical information beyond the
initial clinical evaluation, offering detailed insights into the structure and function
of the respiratory system. In pair with respiratory auscultation, pulmonary func-
tion testing is often the starting point of assessment in the physical examination of
respiratory disease. Spirometry, lung volumes, and diffusing capacity are the most
common techniques to access pulmonary function. Most PFT measures involve the
performance of maximal maneuvers and, therefore, require the patient’s comprehen-
sion, effort, and cooperation with instructions during testing. Sub-optimal efforts
(by the patient) or suboptimal timing will lead to overestimating impairment.
Spirometry: It is a physiological test that measures flow rates and individual in-
halation and exhalation volumes of air as a function of time [46]. It is among the
most useful and commonly available PFTs. Spirometry is invaluable as a general res-
piratory health screening test and can provide simple lung function measurements.
Moreover, spirometric indices are well validated and easily interpreted compared
with well-established normality values [46]. The most frequently used spirometric
measures are Forced vital capacity (FVC) (in liters), which is the largest volume of
air that can be exhaled forcefully from a maximal inhalation, and the Forced expira-
tory volume in 1 second (FEV1) (in liters), which is the volume exhaled during the
first second of a maximal forceful expiratory effort following a maximal inhalation.
The ratio of FEV1/FVC is an important indicator of airflow obstruction, a common
symptom of sevaral respiratory diseases such as asthma or COPD.
This exam is typically used to evaluate the severity of respiratory diseases, such
as COPD and other obstructive diseases [47]. Changes in spirometry values after
bronchodilator administration indicate variable airflow obstruction. However, one

2.2. RESPIRATORY DISEASE ASSESSMENT 33
major limitation of spirometry is its dependence on the patient’s motivation and
cooperation, with a high potential for faulty values if effort is suboptimal [7]. More-
over, it can not provide continuous monitoring of the patients as well.
Lung volumes: Includes the measurement of the total lung capacity; the maximal
volume of air that can be contained in the lung. This requires, in addition to spirom-
etry, indirect measurements, either by plethysmography or wash-out technique, to
estimate air that cannot be exhaled. Reductions in total lung capacity are indicative
of a restrictive ventilatory defect, which may be due to either intrinsic pulmonary
or extra-pulmonary processes. The latter method also allows for measuring airway
resistance.
Diffusing capacity: Refers to the evaluation of the capacity to perform the gas
transfer between air and blood in the lung and is measured as the diffusing lung
capacity for carbon monoxide (DLCO, in ml CO/mmHg/min). This test is usually
performed with a test over a single breathing cycle and measures the overall gas
exchange function of the lung. It requires a maximal inhalation of a test gas followed
by a breath-hold of 10 or 12 seconds.
2.2.3 Imaging Techniques
Imaging tests are pivotal in evaluating the respiratory system, offering a compre-
hensive understanding of its anatomical structure. Techniques such as chest X-rays,
Computerized Tomography (CT) scans, and magnetic resonance imaging (MRI)
provide detailed visualizations of the lungs, airways, and surrounding structures,
helping to detect abnormalities like tumors, infections, and chronic lung diseases.
Chest X-ray: Chest X-ray uses a focused beam of radiation to create an image of
the heart, lungs, blood vessels, airways, and the bones of the chest and spine. Chest
X-rays can also reveal fluid in or around the lungs or air surrounding a lung. A
chest X-ray is often among the first procedures if a healthcare professional suspects
heart or lung disease. Healthcare providers use chest X-rays to diagnose conditions
like pneumonia, COPD, or emphysema.
Chest CT scan: Chest CT allows more detailed visualization of thoracic struc-
tures than plain radiography. It is often performed with intravenous contrast en-
hancement (in suspected pulmonary embolism cases, for example). CT is also helpful
for guiding needle aspiration of peripheral lung lesions. CT scanning has been an im-
portant imaging technique in pulmonary diagnostics for many decades, and specific
imaging techniques, protocols, and applications are continuously evolving.

34 CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND CONCEPTS
Chest magnetic resonance: Magnetic resonance imaging of the chest uses a
powerful magnetic field, radio waves, and a computer to produce detailed pictures
of the structures within the chest. It is primarily used to characterize abnormalities
found on other imaging tests such as chest CT. It is also used to assess the anatomy
and function of the heart and its blood flow.
Ultrasonography: A chest ultrasound is a noninvasive diagnostic exam that pro-
duces images, which are used to assess the organs and structures within the chest,
such as the lungs, mediastinum, and pleural space. Ultrasound technology allows
quick visualization of the chest organs and structures from outside the body. Ultra-
sound may also be used to assess blood flow to chest organs. Chest ultrasonography
can be a useful diagnostic tool for respiratory physicians to assess and monitor res-
piratory pathologies in many different conditions with a wide field of applications.
It is mainly used to investigate pleural effusions, pleural thickening, chest wall ab-
normalities, diagnosing pneumothorax, and biopsies of lesions adjacent to the chest
wall.
2.3 Respiratory Sounds
In general terms, respiratory sounds refer to the specific sounds generated by air
movement through the respiratory airways. These may be easily audible or identi-
fied through auscultation of the respiratory system through the lung fields with a
stethoscope and from the spectral characteristics of lung sounds. The turbulent flow
of air entering and leaving the lungs creates sounds that fall in the human audible
range. In the presence of certain respiratory diseases, these sounds tend to have spe-
cific patterns that can be used as specific biomarkers for diagnosing and monitoring
the diseases (ARS).
Respiratory sounds exhibit different acoustic properties depending on each per-
son’s characteristics (e.g., age, gender, height, position, and airflow), location of
sound acquisition, and position in the respiratory phase [48]. The definition of such
characteristics and mastering the pulmonary auscultation technique by health pro-
fessionals allow objective interpretations of respiratory sound alterations and poten-
tially enhance the early detection, treatment, and monitoring of respiratory diseases.
Moreover, the clear characterization of such sounds boosts the development of com-
puterized methods to analyze respiratory sounds since it creates a set of objective
properties that identify a certain type of sound. Today, most respiratory sound char-
acteristics are obtained from data gathered through computerized acoustic devices,
such as electronic stethoscopes and microphones. Notwithstanding, the methods for
recording, analyzing, and reporting respiratory sounds still differ significantly among
studies, which weakens our understanding of the clinical meaning of these sounds
and the changes in their acoustics. Therefore, keeping updated guidelines on the

2.3. RESPIRATORY SOUNDS 35
recording and further characterization of respiratory sounds is extremely important
to compare results among studies and will ease the integration of the information
provided by respiratory sounds analysis. This development is essential to solidify
and enhance the utility of auscultation at patients’ bedside and further develop
auscultation-based diagnosis and monitoring applications.
In 2000, a task force of the European Respiratory Society (ERS) published a set
of guidelines for research and clinical practice in the field of Computerized Respira-
tory Sound Analysis (CORSA) [42]. These guidelines included an extensive list of
term definitions for respiratory diseases, pulmonary physiology, acoustics, automatic
data handling, and instrumentation. In Figure 2.5, a schematic with the hierarchy
proposed in [42] for categorizing respiratory sounds is presented. According to this
figure, respiratory sounds can be subdivided into three main groups: breath, adven-
titious, and lung sounds. Therefore, those three main groups include breath sounds,
adventitious sounds, cough sounds, snoring sounds, sneezing sounds, and sounds
from the respiratory muscles. Voiced sounds during breathing are not included in
respiratory sounds [42]. Moreover, the sounds can also be divided according to the
region from which they are heard. In general, the current trend is to reduce the
number of categories and standardize the nomenclature [49].

Figure 2.5: Respiratory sounds schematic (adapted from [42]).
Breath sounds According to [42], breath sounds are those arising from breathing,
excluding adventitious sounds heard or recorded over the chest wall, the trachea, or
the mouth. The generation of breath sounds is related to airflow in the respiratory
tract. Acoustically, they are characterized by broad-spectrum noise with a frequency
range depending on the pick-up location.
Adventitious sounds ARS are additional respiratory sounds superimposed on
normal respiratory sounds [42, 45, 48]. There are two main types of adventitious
sounds, Continuous Adventitious Sounds (CAS), and Discontinuous Adventitious
Sounds (DAS).

36 CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND CONCEPTS
Lung sounds Lung sounds concern the respiratory sounds heard or detected over
the chest wall, or within the chest, including breath sounds and adventitious sounds
detected at this location [42,45].
2.3.1 Normal Respiratory Sounds
Normal respiratory sounds are the sounds produced from breathing and can be cate-
gorized based on the location where they are heard or generated, such as the trachea,
chest wall, and mouth [42,43,45,48]. Depending on the auscultation location, differ-
ent types of respiratory sounds have distinct characteristics such as duration, pitch,
and sound quality [43]. Characteristics of normal respiratory sounds also change
from individual to individual because airway dimensions are a function of body
height [50]. However, other factors such as gender, chest location where it is heard,
body position, and airflow can also affect normal respiratory sounds [48].
2.3.1.1 Lung or Vesicular Sounds
Lung or vesicular sounds are the normal breath sounds heard over most of the lung
fields during auscultation. These are produced by air flowing through the small air-
ways and alveoli during inhalation and exhalation. The sound of normal breathing
heard over the surface of the chest is markedly influenced by the anatomical struc-
tures between the site of sound generation and the site of auscultation. The sounds
heard over the chest wall, normal or vesicular lung sounds, are soft, non-musical,
low-pitched, rustling in quality, and are generally only heard during inspiration and
the early part of expiration [33,43,48]. It is believed that turbulent airflow vortexes
generate normal lung sounds at frequencies above 300 Hz; however, the generation
of sounds heard below 300 Hz is not clear [51]. It is well established that the sound
heard during inspiration is produced primarily within the lobar and segmental air-
ways, whereas the sound produced during expiration originates in more proximal
airways. Several mechanisms of vesicular sounds have been suggested, including
turbulent flow, vortexes, and other hitherto unknown mechanisms [33]. Clinically,
a decrease in sound intensity is the most common abnormality. Sound generation
can be decreased when there is a drop in inspiratory airflow, resulting from several
conditions, ranging from poor cooperation to depression of the central nervous sys-
tem [33]. Airway conditions include blockage (e.g., by a foreign body or tumor) and
narrowing in obstructive airway diseases (e.g., asthma and chronic obstructive pul-
monary disease). The decrease in the intensity of breath sounds may be permanent,
as in cases of pure emphysema, or reversible, as in asthma (e.g., during a bronchial
provocation test or an asthma attack).

2.3. RESPIRATORY SOUNDS 37
2.3.1.2 Tracheal Sounds
Tracheal auscultation is not frequently performed (mostly due to the discomfort it
causes in the person being auscultated), but in certain situations, it can convey
important clinical information. Tracheal sounds are breath sounds heard over the
trachea, typically at the front of the neck. These sounds are produced by air moving
through the large airways (the trachea and major bronchi) and are a normal finding
when heard directly over the trachea.
When heard at the suprasternal notch or the lateral neck, normal tracheal sounds
characteristically contain a large amount of sound energy and are easily heard during
the two phases of the respiratory cycle [33]. Normal tracheal sounds are hollow
and clearly heard in both respiratory cycle phases, mostly on inspiration and early
expiration [33, 43, 48]. Their intensity and pitch in the inspiration phase are higher
than in the expiration phase [36]. Typically, they are harsh, loud, and high-pitched
sounds covering a wide range of frequencies, from less than 100 Hz up to 5000 Hz,
with a sharp drop in power at frequencies above 800 Hz and little energy beyond 1500
Hz [36,48]. As mentioned before, these sounds can be heard at the suprasternal notch
and are generated by turbulent airflow in the upper airways, including pharynx,
glottis, and subglottic regions [52]. Listening to tracheal sounds can be useful in
various circumstances [33]. First, the trachea carries sound from within the lungs,
allowing auscultation of other sounds without filtering from the chest cage. Second,
the characteristics of tracheal sounds are similar in quality to the abnormal bronchial
breathing heard in patients with lung consolidation. Third, in patients with upper-
airway obstruction, tracheal sounds can become frankly musical, characterized as a
typical stridor or a localized, intense wheeze.
2.3.1.3 Bronchial Sounds
Normal bronchial sounds are heard over the large airways on the chest, specifically
near the second and third intercostal space. Bronchial sounds are hollower and more
high-pitched than vesicular sounds [36]. Bronchial sounds are audible during both
inspiratory and expiratory phases [43]. In contrast with vesicular sounds, due to
the sounds originating in larger airways, the expiratory phase sounds are normally
audible for longer than the inspiratory phase ones. The intensity of these sounds in
the expiration phase is also higher than in the inspiration phase. Unlike vesicular
sounds, there is a short pause between each breathing cycle.
2.3.1.4 Mouth Sounds
Breath sounds heard from the mouth are produced by central airways and caused
by turbulent airflow below the glottis. Breath sounds from the mouth have a wide
frequency range of 200 to 2000 Hz [53]. The energy distribution is like that of white
noise. For a healthy person, breath sounds from the mouth should be silent.

38 CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND CONCEPTS
2.3.2 Adventitious Respiratory Sounds
As already mentioned, ARS are additional respiratory sounds superimposed on nor-
mal respiratory sounds [33, 36, 42, 45]. These sounds are related to changes within
lung morphology in all airways. They are divided into two main groups: DAS (e.g.,
crackles) and CAS (e.g., wheezes). Usually, the presence of these types of sounds
indicates the existence of a pulmonary disorder [42, 45]. Therefore, they can be
characterized based on the underlying conditions and, hence, be very useful in help-
ing the diagnosis of several diseases [43]. However, the production of ARS is not
exclusive to subjects with respiratory diseases, as healthy individuals might also pro-
duce ARS. In Table 2.1, a summary table presents the main characteristics of the
different types of adventitious sounds. ARS serve as important clinical indicators,
helping healthcare providers detect and diagnose respiratory conditions, assess their
severity, and monitor the effectiveness of treatment.

Continuous Adventitious Sounds
CAS are abnormal breath sounds that have a prolonged, continuous quality and
are typically heard during either inhalation, exhalation, or both. They result from
airflow moving through constricted or narrowed airways and typically last more
than 250 ms [43]. CAS can be further divided according to their pitch, namely as
high-pitched CAS (Wheeze, Stridor, and Gasp) or low-pitched CAS (Rhonchi and
Squawk) [43].
Wheezes and rhonchi The wheezing sound is the most easily discernible ad-
ventitious sound. Wheezes and rhonchi can be heard during inspiration, expiration

2.3. RESPIRATORY SOUNDS 39
(most common), or both phases. While rhonchi are low-pitched CAS, wheezes are
high-pitched [43]. Wheeze is defined as a high-pitched continuous sound with typical
dominant frequencies above 400 Hz, while rhonchi are low-pitched continuous sounds
with a dominant frequency typically below 300 Hz [43, 45]. Wheezes are caused by
airway narrowing, which then causes an airflow limitation; rhonchi are related to
the thickening of mucus in the larger airways [43]. Both sinusoid-like signals might
have up to three harmonic frequencies, with a typical frequency range between 100
Hz and 1000 Hz [43]. Wheezes can also be divided as monophonic or polyphonic,
whether they contain one or more dominant frequencies, respectively [43, 45]. Al-
though many physicians still use the term rhonchi, some prefer to simply refer to
these sounds as low-pitched wheezes [33].
Wheezes are usually louder than the underlying breath sounds and are often
audible at the patient’s open mouth or by auscultation over the trachea, chest wall,
and larynx [48, 52]. On the other hand, rhonchi, being low-pitched, are best heard
over the chest wall [33]. Moreover, the presence of wheezes can reflect acoustical
symptomatic characteristics, not only due to the presence of abnormalities in the
respiratory system but also due to the severity and the location of the most fre-
quently found airway obstructions in asthma and respiratory stenoses [42,45]. Both
wheezes and rhonchi are sounds commonly associated with obstructive diseases, such
as asthma, COPD, and bronchitis [43].

2.3. RESPIRATORY SOUNDS 41
Stridors Stridors are sibilant and musical wheeze-like CAS, with a very loud low-
frequency originating in the larynx or trachea [42,43]. Stridors can mainly be heard
in the inspiration phase, although, on some occasions, they can also be heard on
expiration or even in both phases [33]. Unlike wheezes, the stridor sound is generated
by turbulent airflow in the larynx or bronchial tree and is related to an upper airway
obstruction of the upper respiratory tract, being clearly heard without the aid of a
stethoscope due to its intensity [33,36,43]. Thereby, stridor can mostly be audible on
the trachea, while wheezing can also be heard clearly by chest auscultation. Stridor
sounds typical range is between 500 Hz and 1000 Hz, and they are also normally
harsher and louder than wheeze sounds [33,43,48]. As a type of CAS, stridor sounds
have a typical duration of more than 250 ms.
The differential diagnoses for stridor are epiglottitis, croup, and laryngeal edema.
All of these conditions are related to upper airway obstructions [43]. Stridor sounds
can also be heard when there is a foreign body, such as a tumor, in the upper airway
tract [43].

Squawk Squawks are relatively short, typically inspiratory, CAS with a mixed
constitution, presenting musical and non-musical components [43]. A squawk typ-
ically appears as a short wheeze preceded by a crackle [52]. They have a brief,
squeaky quality and are often described as a combination of crackles and wheezes.
The duration of squawks generally varies between 50 and 400 ms, while their fun-

42 CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND CONCEPTS
damental frequency is usually between 200 and 300 Hz [36, 43, 45]. Squawks are
typically heard from the middle to the end of inspiration in patients with restric-
tive (e.g., allergic alveolitis and diffuse interstitial fibrosis) or acute (e.g., severe
pneumonia and bronchiolitis obliterans) lung pathologies [33,54,55].

Gasp Inspiratory gasps are high-pitched, long-duration sounds that can usually
be heard after a bout of cough when a patient finally tries to inhale. The whooping
sound of an inspiratory gasp is caused by fast-moving air through the respiratory
tract [43]. The whooping sound is a pathognomonic symptom of whooping cough
(pertussis), the only disease associated with this CAS.
Discontinuous Adventitious Sounds
DAS are abnormal sounds characterized by short, intermittent bursts of sound, as
opposed to ARS. These sounds have a short duration, typically less than 25 ms [43].
This category of adventitious sounds is composed of crackles (fine and coarse) and
pleural rubs. Crackles are the main type of DAS, and their evaluation is important
since it can help with the differential diagnosis of several diseases [33,56]. They are
explosive and non-musical sounds, usually classified as fine and coarse crackles based
on their duration, loudness, pitch, timing in the respiratory cycle, and relationship to
coughing and changing body position [36,45]. Besides crackles’ frequency, duration,

2.3. RESPIRATORY SOUNDS 43
gravity (in)dependence, (non)response to forced expiratory maneuvers, and timing
within the respiratory cycle, their number and the anatomical place from where they
are auscultated should also be considered. As several diseases progress, crackles tend
to increase in number and occur first in the basal areas and later in the upper areas of
the lungs [48,52]. In addition to the described crackles’ characteristics, some studies
have also used crackles’ Initial Deflection Width (IDW), Largest Deflection Width
(LDW), and Two-Cycle Duration (2CD) for diagnostic and monitoring purposes
[45,48].
Fine crackle Fine crackle sounds are caused by explosive openings of the small
airways and are produced within the small airways [36, 43]. The sound is high-
pitched (around 650 Hz) and has a short duration (around 5 ms) [43]. Fine crackles
are usually audible only at the late stages of inspiratory phases (mid-to-late) and are
usually associated with pneumonia, congestive heart failure, and lung fibrosis [43].

Coarse crackle Coarse crackle sounds are generated by air bubbles in large
bronchi [43]. The sounds can be heard mostly during the early stages of inspira-
tion but are also audible at the expiratory stage. Coarse crackles have a “popping”
quality with a low pitch, around 350 Hz, and a duration of around 15 ms [43].
They may be heard over any lung region, are usually transmitted to the mouth,
can change or disappear with coughing, and are not influenced by changes in body

44 CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND CONCEPTS
position [33]. They are commonly associated with patients suffering from chronic
bronchitis, bronchiectasis, COPD, and severe pulmonary edema [36].

Pleural rub In healthy persons, the parietal and visceral pleura slide over each
other silently [33]. However, in persons with various lung-related diseases, the vis-
ceral pleura can become rough enough that its passage over the parietal pleura pro-
duces crackling sounds heard as a friction rub [33]. Pleural rubs are often painful,
as the inflamed pleurae are highly sensitive, and patients may feel sharp, localized
chest pain that worsens with deep breaths or coughing. Pleural rub sounds are coarse
crackles arising from parietal and visceral pleura rubbing against each other [42].
The sounds are heard in both respiratory phases (biphasic) and are non-musical
and explosive sounds [33]. They have a low pitch, normally below 350 Hz, and are
mostly caused by inflammation of the pleural membrane [36,43].

2.3.3 Diagnostic Value of Respiratory Sounds
Respiratory complaints are among the most common causes that bring a patient
to the doctor [32]. Consequently, respiratory sounds, either heard at a distance or
auscultated over the chest, are integral to the patient’s evaluation and may pro-
vide valuable clues regarding their pathology [32]. Thus, these sounds are signifi-
cant as they provide valuable information regarding the respiratory system’s overall
health, making auscultation valuable to extract information during physical exam-
ination [57]. Additionally, an auscultation-based diagnosis of pulmonary disorders
relies heavily on adventitious sounds and the chest wall’s altered transmission char-
acteristics [32,58]. Thereby, adventitious respiratory sounds provide crucial informa-
tion on respiratory dysfunction, and changes in their characteristics (e.g., intensity,
duration, timing) might inform the clinical course of respiratory diseases and treat-
ments [7].
Breath sounds may be abnormal in certain airways or lung pathological condi-
tions. Breath sounds with abnormally high frequencies and intensity are typically
observed in many diseases with airway obstruction, like asthma and chronic bron-
chitis [42]. In a population study [59], the authors have shown that changes in lung
sound spectra could help in the early detection of airway disease, enhancing the
diagnostic value of respiratory sounds.
A model [60] showed that wheezes are always accompanied by flow limitation,
but that flow limitation is not necessarily accompanied by wheezes. Wheezing is
common in asthma and a common clinical sign in patients with obstructive airway

46 CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND CONCEPTS
diseases. Another study [61] has demonstrated an association between the degree of
bronchial obstruction and the presence and characteristics of wheezes. The strongest
association was obtained when the degree of bronchial obstruction was compared to
the proportion of the respiratory cycle occupied by wheezing.
Crackles occur frequently in cardio-respiratory diseases. The acoustic character-
istics and timing of crackles depend on the disease being studied. When present,
crackling sounds in patients with lung fibrosis are typically fine, repetitive, and end-
inspiratory, whereas those associated with chronic airway obstruction (e.g., COPD,
emphysema or bronchiectasis) are coarse, less repeatable, and occur early in inspi-
ration. The appearance of crackles may be an early sign of respiratory disease [42].
When present, the number of crackles per breath is associated with the severity of
the disease in patients with interstitial lung disorders [62]. Moreover, the waveform
and timing of crackles may have clinical significance in the differential diagnosis of
cardio-respiratory disorders. Another study found that early inspiratory crackles
predicted COPD more strongly than coarse inspiratory crackles [56]. The same
study also suggested that the identification of early crackles at the lung bases was
strongly correlated with COPD [56].
Table 2.2 summarizes some of the most common respiratory diseases associated
with different adventitious sounds.
