Overall, ARS, such as wheezes, crackles, rhonchi, and pleural rubs, hold signifi-
cant clinical importance as they provide valuable clues about underlying respiratory
conditions. These abnormal breath sounds, heard during auscultation, often indicate
changes within the airways or lung tissue, such as inflammation, fluid buildup, air-
way narrowing, or structural alterations. Recognizing these sounds can help health-
care providers detect respiratory abnormalities early, monitor disease progression,
and evaluate the effectiveness of treatment, making adventitious sounds crucial in

2.4. COMPUTERIZED RESPIRATORY SOUND ANALYSIS (CORSA) 47
respiratory assessment and diagnosis.
For example, wheezes suggest airway constriction, commonly associated with
asthma or COPD. At the same time, crackles may indicate fluid or fibrosis in the
lungs, often seen in conditions like pneumonia, pulmonary edema, or interstitial lung
disease.
2.4 Computerized Respiratory Sound Analysis (CORSA)
Auscultation with a stethoscope has been an essential tool for diagnosing patients
suffering from respiratory disease. Although auscultation is non-invasive, rapid, and
inexpensive, it has intrinsic limitations such as inter-listener variability and sub-
jectivity, and the examination must be performed face-to-face. Moreover, it does
not allow the user to store the listened audio in a digital format for later playback.
Automated analysis of respiratory sounds has emerged as a promising solution, lever-
aging advances in signal processing and machine learning. These technologies can
enhance the detection, classification, and interpretation of respiratory sounds, offer-
ing a more standardized and reliable approach to respiratory health assessment. By
converting acoustic signals into digital data, signal processing techniques enable the
extraction of key features from respiratory sounds, such as frequency, amplitude,
and timing. These features can then be used to identify abnormal patterns, namely
ARS, associated with specific respiratory conditions.
Computerized Respiratory Sound Analysis (CORSA) is an emerging technol-
ogy aimed at enhancing the detection, classification, and monitoring of respiratory
sounds using digital signal processing and machine learning algorithms. In general
terms, CORSA consists of recording patients’ respiratory sounds with an electronic
device and their subsequent analysis based on specific signal characteristics. It is a
simple, objective, and noninvasive method to detect and characterize adventitious
respiratory sounds (i.e., crackles and wheezes) or other signal-specific characteris-
tics [7,44]. CORSA methods have several advantages when compared to traditional
clinician-performed auscultation, namely, better compliance with objective criteria
that define normal/abnormal respiratory sound, reduced inter-listener variability,
and improved diagnostic yield of pulmonary disorders [44, 64]. A block diagram of
a lung sound acquisition, recording, and analysis system is depicted in Figure 2.14.
Figure 2.14: Overview of lung sound data acquisition, recording, and computerized anal-
ysis system (image from [38]).
CORSA based technologies are particularly valuable due to the potential to be

48 CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND CONCEPTS
integrated with wearable devices or telemedicine platforms, allowing for continuous,
remote monitoring of patients in various settings. By offering a more precise, stan-
dardized approach, CORSA has the potential to improve diagnostic accuracy, assist
in early intervention, and streamline respiratory health management, especially in
regions with limited access to specialized healthcare.
In order to standardize the measurements protocols and terms used in respiratory
sound computerized analysis, the European Respiratory Societyfinanced a BIOMED
Concerted Action project entitled “Computerized Respiratory Sound Analysis” that
proposed to define a semiology adapted to collect and analyze respiratory sounds
[42,65]. From the CORSA project, a set of guidelines was published. In Table 2.3, a
summary of the CORSA guidelines is presented. Nevertheless, currently, there are
still no standardized methods for recording, analyzing, and classifying respiratory
sounds, as these methods are highly variable across studies.
Table 2.3: Summary of CORSA recommendations for the acquisition of respiratory
sounds, namely characteristics of sensors and microphone locations (adapted from [37]).

To record breath sounds, there are basically two major types of sensors that can
be used: 1) Contact sensors, for direct recording of the movement of the chest wall
(e.g., piezoelectric accelerometer); 2) Air-coupled sensors for acoustic recording of
the movement of a diaphragm exposed to the air pressure generated by the chest-
wall movement (e.g., condenser, in particular, electric microphone) [38]. Depending
on the type of sensor used, different methods are considered to attach them to the
body, as seen in Table 2.3. The CORSA task force has also settled some of the most
relevant locations to perform the recording of respiratory sounds, such as the trachea
(for tracheal sounds) and left and right posterior base of the lungs, usually 5 cm

2.5. ELECTRICAL IMPEDANCE TOMOGRAPHY (EIT) 49
laterally from the paravertebral line and 7 cm below the scapular angle for adults [37].
Later on, multichannel recording systems were developed in various laboratories, and
multichannel analysis of respiratory sounds became increasingly more popular, since
the simultaneous recording and analysis of sounds from multiple sites over the chest
facilitated and accelerated an otherwise slow and tedious process [38]. Other optional
locations have been recommended by CORSA for adults, such as the right and left
anterior area of the chest at the second intercostal space at the mid-clavicular line
and the right and left lateral area of the chest at the fourth or fifth intercostal space
on the mid-axillary line. In tracheal breath measurements, the sensor is usually
attached to the neck at the anterior cervical triangle [38].
There are also a number of recommendations by the CORSA Task Force appli-
cable to the environmental and subject conditions during breath sound recording,
namely [38]:
• The background noise level should preferably be less than 45 dB (A) and 60
dB (linear) with minimum ambient noise from the environment, including voiced
sounds. The sounds induced by airflow through the flow transducer should be
taken into account.
• The recording should be performed at comfortable room temperature, humidity,
lighting, and ventilation conditions.
• The sitting position is preferable for the subject for short-term recording, whereas
the supine position is preferable for long-term recording.
• For adults, tidal breathing of 7–10 respiratory cycles, with a peak expiratory and
inspiratory flow of 1–1.5 l/s or 10–15% of the predicted maximum peak flow,
and tidal volume of 1 L or 15–20% of predicted vital capacity are recommended
for short-term recording, whereas tidal breathing without any voluntary effort is
recommended for long-term recording.
• The airflow, volume, and flow/volume display should be, whenever possible, in
front of the subject.
• For babies and young children, chest movement monitoring by strain gauge bands,
pneumatic belts, or chest straps should be used.
2.5 Electrical Impedance Tomography (EIT)
EIT is a radiation-free, real-time imaging technique that reconstructs the internal
conductivity distribution of a subject based on electrical stimulation and measure-
ments on the body surface [66]. In thoracic EIT, this technique is based on the
concept of bioimpedance, which is described as the ability to impede electric current
by a biological tissue. Biological tissues consist of cells suspended in extracellular
fluid. Each cell has a membrane enveloping intracellular fluid. Both the intracellular

50 CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND CONCEPTS
and extracellular fluids are highly conductive and provide resistive paths. The lipid
bilayer cell membrane, which separates the intracellular and extracellular fluids, acts
as a dielectric and provides capacitive reactance [66]. The electrical properties of
biological tissues have been widely studied in the literature, and different tissues
conduct electricity better than others because they contain more ions, leading to
measurable resistivity differences between tissue types in the body. In Table 2.4, the
typical resistivity values for different body tissues are presented.
Table 2.4: Typical values of tissue resistivity at a frequency of about 10 kHz (adapted
from [67]).
Tissue Resistivity (Ω.m)
Muscle 2-4
Fat 20
Lungs 10 (highly variable with respiration)
Bone 1.6
Blood >40
EIT has been a hot topic among researchers for the last 30 years. Besides having
applications in geophysics and industrial process monitoring, over the past decades
EIT has been the subject of significant intensive research and has proven to be a
reliable tool for monitoring physiological processes in the human body, such as car-
diorespiratory and brain activity, for instance, [68–70]. Figure 2.15 represents some
of the most relevant areas of application for the EIT imaging modality, including
industrial and clinical ones.
EIT
Geophysics
Industrial 
Processes
Medical Imaging
Gastric-
Emptying
Head Imaging
Breast Imaging
Lung Imaging
Neonates
Intensive Care
Portable, Real 
Time Monitoring
Figure 2.15: EIT application areas (based on [71]).
While conventional medical tomography techniques are expensive, require large
hospital spaces, and emit radiation, EIT is an attractive alternative with several ad-
vantages: it does not use ionizing radiation, is non-invasive, is characterized by high

2.5. ELECTRICAL IMPEDANCE TOMOGRAPHY (EIT) 51
temporal resolution, and it is potentially inexpensive easy to use [66]. The ability
of EIT to provide dynamic, real-time imaging makes it a promising tool for respi-
ratory monitoring, offering insights that enhance patient management, particularly
in critical care and other settings requiring continuous lung assessment. Moreover,
the possibility to design wearable devices based on EIT has recently given a boost
to this technology, which may represent a unique wearable imaging technique. De-
spite several advantages when compared to traditionally established medical imag-
ing modalities, EIT’s major limitations include lower spatial resolution compared
to other imaging techniques (such as echography, magnetic resonance imaging, and
CT), the large inter-subject variability and the artifacts derived from the electrodes’
movement and poor contact quality [66]. Figure 2.16 presents a side-by-side compar-
ison between the anatomical resolution of EIT against CT scans. While with EIT,
it is not possible to determine precise anatomical details, it is possible to identify
regions where lesions occur, as corroborated by the CT scan images.
(a) EIT tidal image (left) and corresponding CT image (right) of a patient with pleural effusion in
the left lung.
(b) EIT tidal image (left) and corresponding CT image (right) of a patient with a pneumothorax
in the left lung.
Figure 2.16: Comparison of anatomical resolution between EIT and CT scan (adapted
from [72]).
Usually, EIT devices can be found in advanced Intensive Care Units (ICUs) as
bedside monitor equipment. Dr¨ ager, Timpel, and Sentec (formerly Swisstom) are
three of the most relevant EIT device manufacturers, with their models PulmoVista

52 CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND CONCEPTS
500, Enlight 2100 and BB2, respectively. Nonetheless, several research efforts have
been made in order to develop wearable EIT devices that would allow for patients’
continuous monitoring [73–78]. Figure 2.17 present a typical bed-side EIT device.
(a) Dr¨ ager PulmoVista500 EIT equipment.
 (b) EIT electrodes belt.
Figure 2.17: Dr¨ ager PulmoVista500 EIT equipment and electrode belt (images from [72]).
This relatively new imaging modality has been evolving over the last few decades,
gaining particular importance in lung function monitoring [71,79]. In the clinical res-
piratory setting, EIT is almost exclusively used to examine the distribution of lung
ventilation and regional aeration changes in critically ill, typically mechanically ven-
tilated lying patients [80]. Several reasons justify the investment in research that
has been made around EIT lung monitoring. First, EIT provides images based on
different physical characteristics than other imaging techniques, i.e., it is based on
electrical tissue properties. Another important aspect is that EIT, in contrast to
all other tomographic imaging techniques, does not apply any ionizing radiation,
and there are no known hazards for the patient, as the currents applied are rel-
atively small [81]. Therefore, EIT can provide a non-invasive, radiation-free, and
continuous image of pulmonary impedance, indicating the ventilation distribution.
This makes EIT an ideal candidate for bedside monitoring of lung development of
pre-term neonates, patient monitoring in ICUs, and portable continuous monitoring
of patients during a long period of time. EIT can also assist in optimizing ventila-
tor settings by providing real-time feedback on regional lung ventilation, helping to
avoid over-inflation and under-inflation of lung areas
The main idea behind EIT is to turn the impedance within a body into 2D/3D
images, using specific electrical stimulation patterns and the respective measure-
ments on the surface of interest. Thus, in EIT, the internal conductivity distribu-
tion of the subject is reconstructed based on electrical measurements from electrodes

2.5. ELECTRICAL IMPEDANCE TOMOGRAPHY (EIT) 53
attached around the boundary of the subject of interest [82]. An EIT system is
composed of two essential blocks: 1) data acquisition hardware and 2) image re-
construction software [82]. Therefore, every EIT system usually has the following
generic components:
• An array of electrodes, which is attached to the surface of interest;
• A current generator that is used for generating a constant sinusoidal alternating
current to inject into the surface of interest, with different stimulation patterns;
• An image reconstruction algorithm, which reconstructs images of the internal
resistivity distribution of the object from the voltage measurements;
• A computer (display, storage, etc.) that is used to enable easy and user-friendly
access to the image analysis.
The typical steps involved in EIT data acquisition, processing, and analysis are
represented in Figure 2.18.
Figure 2.18: Sequence of steps involved in EIT data analysis - Typical pipeline (image
from [83]).
Raw data/measurements EIT devices interact with the body using an electric
stimulation current across a set of electrodes connected to the exterior of the patient.
Typically, the current is applied from an alternating current source to a pair of
electrodes while voltage differences are measured on all the remaining electrode
pairs [71, 79]. The set of impedance values acquired during one cycle of current
application in all injecting electrode pairs is called a frame, which will correspond to
one single raw image [79]. Currently, many techniques can be used to inject current
into the electrodes [82].
Image reconstruction Image reconstruction is generating raw EIT images from
the measured voltages, typically from a two-dimensional slice through the electrode
plane. Developing new reconstruction algorithms is an active research field in EIT,
with advances in image quality achieved by using, for example, a priori anatomical
information.
The change in the relative impedance in each pixel is used to reconstruct the
information provided by the impedance values. This (dimensionless) value is de-
rived from the difference in tissue impedance between two instants in time or be-
tween inspiration and expiration (expressed in arbitrary units-AU) [84]. From the

54 CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND CONCEPTS
reconstructed images (Figure 2.19), it is possible to obtain the Global Impedance
Curve (GIC) by summing each frame pixel values, which corresponds to the total
impedance value represented over time (see Figure 2.20). Figure 2.19 shows the
ventilation distribution over time in reconstructed EIT frames of an healthy sub-
ject. Well-ventilated lung regions exhibit high resistivity variation (yellow), and
conversely, regions with absent ventilation are depicted as areas of low variation
(blue). Figure 2.20 also highlights one of EIT major capabilities, which is the ability
to provide a regional assessment of specifics Region of Interests (ROIs), in this case,
the right and left lungs.
Figure 2.19: Series of raw EIT reconstructed frames over time (∆Z (A.U.) - impedance
variation in arbitrary units).
EIT waveforms and ROIs EIT data analysis is based on the EIT waveforms
that are generated from a series of raw EIT images in individual image pixels [79].
The ROI can be defined in an image as a specific set of pixels to study (minimum size
being one image pixel). In each ROI, the waveform over time displays the short-
term and long-term changes in local electrical impedance resulting from various
physiological or pathological effects. Periodic signal fluctuations are induced by
ventilation or by heart action and lung perfusion. Digital frequency filtering is often
used in EIT data analysis to isolate these periodic phenomena.
EIT lung ROIs are mostly functionally identified as regions where ventilation-
related impedance changes occur. If this approach is applied, lung regions with
absent or small ventilation-related impedance changes may be missed. The other
motivation for ROI-based analysis of EIT data is to characterize the spatial hetero-
geneity of lung ventilation. For this application, arbitrary ROIs, like image quad-

2.5. ELECTRICAL IMPEDANCE TOMOGRAPHY (EIT) 55
Figure 2.20: EIT GIC and regional impedance curves for right and left lungs (∆Z (A.U.)
- impedance variation in arbitrary units).
rants or layers, are typically used.
EIT measures EIT related measures are determined from the analysis of the EIT
images [79]. Typically, EIT related measures are used to assess the ventilation and
perfusion state of the lungs. In broader terms, quantitative EIT measures can be
divided into three groups, namely average global/regional measures, measures of
characterization for spatial distribution, and exam-specific measures.
Average regional/global EIT measures are those representing the average (or
sum) of Functional EIT (fEIT) measures. These measures are typically used to
characterize the overall ventilation and aeration and the changes in these quantities,
such as the tidal volume and changes in end-expiratory lung volume. These measures
can be determined using the whole image (global) or using only certain regions of
interest (regional), like the left lung, for instance [79].
Measures of the spatial distribution of ventilation are measures used to char-
acterize the spatial distribution of the ventilated region. These measures typically
include ventilation heterogeneity-related variables.
Exam-specific measures characterize aspects of lung physiology using specific
examinations or measurements of EIT in parallel with other signals, such as airway
pressure. This is a varied group of EIT measures, which is typically computed from
pixel functional measures [79].
A series of EIT based features have been proposed and linked with pathologies
and diagnosis in the literature. These entail features calculated in tidal breath-
ing, e.g., Global Inhomogeneity (GI) index, center of ventilation, or during forced
maneuvers, e.g., spatial distribution of FEV1/FVC and other pulmonary function-

56 CHAPTER 2. BACKGROUND CONCEPTS
related features. Moreover, with EIT, it is also possible to detect end-expiratory
and end-inspiratory EIT frames during spontaneous tidal breathing and estimate
the respiratory rate. With those instances, fEIT can be computed. fEIT images are
generated from a series of raw images and the corresponding pixel EIT waveforms,
using appropriate mathematical equations in each image pixel [79]. Various types
of fEIT images have been proposed in the literature [79].
2.6 Summary
Respiratory diseases can substantially lower the patient’s quality of life, as they are
often very limiting diseases. Many of them are often incurable, such as chronic
respiratory diseases; however, various forms of treatment that help dilate major air
passages and improve shortness of breath can help manage symptoms and improve
quality of life. Throughout the years, despite their limitations, auscultation, spirom-
etry, and medical imaging remained the gold standard methods to assess this type of
condition. While these techniques are well established in clinical practice, they are
unsuitable for continuous and remote monitoring, namely in telehealth applications.
To overcome some of the limitations of conventional techniques, other method-
ologies, such as the computerized analysis of respiratory sounds and EIT, have been
the focus of many research projects, aiming at more objective and autonomous meth-
ods to diagnose and monitor respiratory conditions. Although these methods are
not fully matured yet, they are striving towards a future where they can surpass
the limitations of traditional techniques. Merging the two data sources also holds
great promise, as they can model important aspects of the respiratory function and
provide complementary information.

T
his chapter presents an overview of the main thematic trends in the
literature concerning the automatic analysis of respiratory sounds and
Electrical Impedance Tomography (EIT). Before the current thesis, no
work was conducted using lung sounds and EIT together, nor were
there publicly available databases containing lung sounds and EIT data. The fusion
of these two data sources can bring several advantages and help improve the diag-
nosis and monitoring of respiratory conditions as they model two critical aspects
of respiratory function: the sounds produced while breathing and the respiratory
function itself. Notwithstanding, several difficulties and technical constraints must
be overcome before this data fusion approach becomes a reality.
The chapter is divided into two main sections: one focused on respiratory sounds
and the other on EIT. In the respiratory sound section, an overview of the primary
tasks explored in the literature is provided, along with a discussion of several relevant
57

58 CHAPTER 3. LITERATURE REVIEW
works within each task. Additionally, the open-access databases available in the
literature are reviewed. Conversely, the EIT section presents various clinical use
cases of EIT, as well as studies leveraging this biosignal for the classification and
characterization of respiratory diseases.
3.1 Respiratory Sound Analysis
Since Laennec first invented the stethoscope in 1819, this new instrument quickly be-
came common among all doctors, allowing them to listen and investigate the sounds
produced by the heart and lungs [85]. Currently, chest auscultation performed by
physicians remains one of the most effective tools for diagnosing respiratory dis-
orders. This type of auscultation-based diagnosis heavily relies on the presence of
adventitious sounds and the chest wall’s altered transmission characteristics [58].
In recent years, many works have been developed looking for new computerized
techniques to diagnose and monitor respiratory diseases. Automatic detection and
classification of adventitious sounds are among the most valuable manners to assist
physicians in diagnosing or monitoring diseases such as asthma, Chronic obstructive
pulmonary disease (COPD), and pneumonia [43]. However, despite the increasing
number of studies regarding computerized respiratory sound analysis, specifically
for detecting or classifying adventitious sounds, a standardized approach and com-
parison have not been well established [43].
3.1.1 Pioneering Works in Respiratory Sound Analysis
The automated analysis of respiratory sounds represents a pivotal advancement in
medical diagnostics, with its roots tracing back to the mid-20th century. Early
pioneers in this field sought to develop techniques that could assist clinicians in
identifying and diagnosing respiratory disorders, such as asthma, pneumonia, and
COPD, by analyzing lung sounds with greater precision and consistency. Initial
efforts were driven by manual auscultation limitations, which are subjective and
depend on the clinician’s expertise. One of the earliest breakthroughs came with
the application of digital signal processing techniques to record and analyze sounds
like wheezes, crackles, and stridor, laying the groundwork for future developments
in electronic stethoscopes and computer-aided diagnosis systems. These early works
established key algorithms for sound pattern recognition and opened new avenues
for non-invasive, objective, and scalable respiratory health monitoring.
Using spectral analysis, Urquhart et al. [86] discriminated between a group of
healthy subjects and three groups of patients with different pulmonary diseases.
They divided the frequency range of 0-400 Hz into 20 intervals and produced a piece-
wise constant approximation to the spectral envelopes. Subsequently, a Karhunen-
Loeve transform was applied to extract the two most significant variables of the

3.1. RESPIRATORY SOUND ANALYSIS 59
20-dimensional feature space. These two variables were plotted to analyze the dif-
ferences between the four groups. In another approach to discriminate between
respiratory sounds of healthy and pathological subjects, auto-regressive models of
different orders were fed to the k-nearest neighbor classifier, and their performance
was compared [87].
Holford [88] used a balanced sample of 12 fine and coarse crackles to build a
discriminant curve based on two features and the Mahalanobis distance. The dis-
criminant curve was used to classify all 6 test set samples successfully. The first
validated method for detecting crackles was introduced by Murphy et al. [89], but
the details of its implementation were not revealed. Kaisia et al. [90] proposed a
crackle detection method based on the analysis of the spectral stationarity of lung
sounds. Du et al. [91] proposed a mathematical model for crackles based on the crite-
ria for an ideal crackle. Using this model as the mother wavelet, they used matched
wavelet analysis to detect crackles in a sample of lung sounds from a teaching tape.
Hadjileontiadis and Panas [92] proposed a wavelet-based filter for separating discon-
tinuous adventitious sounds from normal respiratory sounds.
Forkheim et al. [93] presented an analysis of the use of Artificial Neural Networks
(ANNs) to discriminate between isolated 100 ms segments containing wheezes and
segments with no wheezes. They compared the performance of several ANNs on two
small groups of patients, not revealing the number of patients.
3.1.2 Respiratory Sound Databases
Despite the analysis of respiratory sound being a topic of interest for a long time
in the literature, as highlighted in the previous section, most pioneer works were
conducted on small-scale datasets, with only a few examples, and typically collected
in controlled environments. In fact, until recently, one of the core problems in the
field of respiratory sound analysis was the lack of large publicly available databases
that can serve to develop algorithms and benchmark results. Since most works in
respiratory sound analysis and processing are based on machine learning approaches,
having large and representative databases is essential for algorithmic development.
Otherwise, developing methods with good generalization capacity that work well
in “real-world” conditions is harder. In their systematic review, Pramono et al.
identified the data source for all the 77 articles covered [43]. Even though most works
have used private data collections, 13 publicly available databases were among the
data sources. These represent four online repositories and nine audio CD companion
books. After the review by Pramono, the field of automated respiratory sound
analysis has expanded significantly and experienced a growing interest with the
release of several open-access databases with respiratory sound. Table 3.1) presents
a summary of the current publicly available databases, which includes several listed
by Pramono and others recently made available.

3.1. RESPIRATORY SOUND ANALYSIS 63
Until recently, the most commonly used databases in the literature were small
repositories with typical “text-book” examples. Since most of these repositories
were designed for teaching purposes, they only include a small number of examples
of each type of respiratory sound. Moreover, most of these sounds are clear and
do not include environmental noise, which is commonly present in clinical practice.
Thus, they are unsuitable for developing realistic models that aim to be deployed in
“real-world” applications in the context of respiratory sound automated analysis and
processing. Below is a summary description of the current open-access respiratory
sound databases.
In 2017, to overcome these difficulties, the Respiratory Sound Database (RSD)
[105,106] was compiled for the scientific challenge at the International Conference on
Biomedical and Health Informatics (ICBHI) 2017. The RSD significantly changed
the automated respiratory sound processing landscape. At the time of publishing, it
was the largest publicly available database in the area, with data collected with mul-
tiple acquisition devices (3M Littmann Classic II SE Stethoscope, 3M Littmann 3200
Electronic Stethoscope, WelchAllyn Meditron Master Elite Electronic Stethoscope,
and a condenser microphone AKG C417L Microphone) under varied environmental
setups with high noise levels, mimicking the real hospital environment. The database
contains 920 recordings from 126 subjects (adults and children), with more than 5.5
hours of recording, and it was collected in two countries, Portugal and Greece, by two
independent research teams over several years. The database also contained single
and multi-channel audio recordings (collected at multiple positions simultaneously).
Most of the database consists of audio recordings made by the research team at the
Faculty of Health Sciences of the University of Aveiro (ESSUA), collected at the
Research and Rehabilitation Respiratory Laboratory (Lab3R) of ESSUA and at the
Infante D. Pedro Hospital in Aveiro, Portugal. The second research team, consist-
ing of Aristotle University of Thessaloniki (AUTH) and the University of Coimbra
(UC), acquired respiratory sounds at the General Hospital of Papanikolaou, Thes-
saloniki, Greece, and at the General Hospital of Imathia (Health Unit of Naousa),
Greece. The RSD database contains several types of annotations, namely Adven-
titious Respiratory Sounds (ARS) events annotations, respiratory cycles temporal
onsets annotations, and the presence of ARS, or not, in those cycles. In total, the
database contains 8877 crackle events and 1898 wheeze events. Regarding respira-
tory cycles, the database contains 1864 cycles with crackles, 886 with wheezes, 506
with both wheezes and crackles, and 3639 without ARS.
Since the RSD was made available, the database was widely adopted, and the
number of works in respiratory sound analysis has experienced a steady increase,
leveraging the amount of data available. Moreover, it has allowed researchers to ex-
plore new approaches to analyze and process respiratory sounds, particularly using
data-driven approaches, such as Deep Learning (DL), and exploring new challenges
uncharted until then. In turn, this has led to a significant push by the research com-

64 CHAPTER 3. LITERATURE REVIEW
munity to increase their efforts to gather and compile new databases with respiratory
sound.
With the growing interest in respiratory sound analysis, several open-access
databases followed in the upcoming years after the release of the RSD. In 2020,
a group of Turkish researchers published an open-access database containing respi-
ratory sound recordings collected by two pulmonologist clinicians in Antakya State
Hospital. The RespiratoryDatabase@TR [108] is a multi-media respiratory database
containing 50 subjects (25 healthy and 25 suffering from COPD). To each subject
suffering from COPD, a degree of severity, ranging from 0 to 4, was assigned using
clinical metrics, chest X-ray, and pulmonary function test. The database contains
600 respiratory sounds recorded with a 12-channel recording device.
In 2021, another research group in Jordan also published a new respiratory sound
database focused on classifying respiratory diseases [109,119]. The dataset includes
respiratory sounds from 112 subjects (35 healthy and 77 unhealthy. The unhealthy
subjects suffered from various respiratory diseases, such as COPD, asthma, pneu-
monia, and lung fibrosis, among others. The recordings were performed using the
3M TM Littmann ® Electronic Stethoscope model 3200, and 112 audios were col-
lected, one per subject at different locations of the chest (upper, mid, lower). On
a recording level, the database also contains annotations for the presence of adven-
titious sounds at the recording level, namely crackles and wheezes. In total, the
database contains 35 recordings without abnormal sounds, 23 with crepitations, 41
with wheezes, 8 with crackles, 1 with bronchial sounds, 2 with wheezes and crackles,
and 2 with bronchial sounds and crackles.
Also, in 2021, a group of researchers from Taiwan started rolling out several
versions of their respiratory sound database, the HF database. In total, they have
published 3 different databases, HF Lung V1 [111], HF Lung V2 [112] (incremental
version of HF Lung V1), HF Tracheal V1 [113]. Currently, these are the largest
open-access repositories of respiratory sound. The dataset HF Lung V2 comprises
13957 audio files (9765 - HF Lung V1) from 300 subjects (261 - HF Lung V1). This
database contains single-channel sequential recordings at different chest positions
(with the Littmann 3200, 3M electronic stethoscope) and multi-channel recordings
(with the HF-Type-1 device, customized multichannel acoustic recording device).
The HF Tracheal V1 contains 10448 15-s tracheal sound recordings collected with
the HF-Type-2 and HF-Type-3 custom devices from 227 participants undergoing
a diagnostic/surgical procedure under monitored anesthesia care. The databases
contain event-level annotations of CAS, DAS, and respiratory phases. In total, the
HF Lung V1 contains 34095 inhalation labels, 18349 exhalation labels, 13883 CAS
labels (8457 wheeze labels, 686 stridor labels, and 4740 rhonchus labels), and 15606
DAS labels (all crackles). The HF Lung V2 contains 49373 inhalation labels, 24552
exhalation labels, 21558 CAS labels (13139 wheeze labels, 914 stridor labels, and
7505 rhonchus labels), and 19617 DAS labels (all crackles). Lastly, HF Tracheal V1

3.1. RESPIRATORY SOUND ANALYSIS 65
contains the 21741 inhalation labels, 15858 exhalation labels, and 6414 CAS labels.
In a similar fashion to the RSD/ICBHI 2017 database, in 2023, a database con-
taining respiratory sounds, the SPRSound: Open-Source SJTU Paediatric Respira-
tory Sound Database [114], was compiled and released as part of a conference chal-
lenge, the IEEE BioCAS 2023 Grand Challenge. Unlike previous large open-access
databases, the SPRSound database focuses solely on the pediatric demographic. In
total, it comprises 292 pediatric participants with a wide range of respiratory dis-
eases and 2683 recordings of respiratory sounds. The data was collected at the pedi-
atric respiratory department of Shanghai Children’s Medical Center with a Yunting
model II digital stethoscope. The database contains 9089 event-level annotations
of abnormal respiratory events, CAS and DAS, as well as normal events. These
are divided into 9089 respiratory sound events: 6887 normal events, 53 rhonchi,
865 wheezes, 17 stridors, 66 coarse crackles, 1167 fine crackles, and 34 wheezes and
crackles events. The database also contains annotations regarding the audio quality
of the recordings.
In 2023, the same research group that published the RSD released the Bi-
modal Repository of Auscultation Coupled with Electrical Impedance Thoracic Sig-
nals (BRACETS) [115]. The BRACETS database is the first open-access bimodal
database containing respiratory sound and EIT. This database was compiled as
part of this PhD thesis. The resulting database consists of 1097 respiratory sounds
and 795 EIT recordings acquired from 78 adult subjects in two countries (Portugal
and Greece). The recorded subjects suffered from various respiratory pathologies,
with several of them enrolled in a respiratory rehabilitation program. Respiratory
sound data were recorded using the 3M Littmann Electronic Stethoscope 3200, and
EIT data were collected using the Goe-MF II EIT device (CareFusion, H¨ ochberg,
Germany). Each subject contains several respiratory sound recordings at multiple
points of the chest. Respiratory sound and EIT were collected simultaneously for
each recording. The database also contains both single and dual-channel sound
recordings.
Lastly, in 2024, a new open-access database containing artificially generated res-
piratory sounds was publicly released [117,118]. In this dataset, a 3M ™ Littmann®
CORE Digital Stethoscope is used to capture both heart and lung sounds, includ-
ing individual and mixed recordings. The recordings were collected from a clinical
manikin (CAE Juno™), a patient simulator designed to replicate human physiolog-
ical conditions, generating clean heart and lung sounds at different body locations.
The dataset includes both normal sounds and various abnormalities (i.e., murmur,
atrial fibrillation, tachycardia, atrioventricular block, third and fourth heart sound,
wheezing, crackles, rhonchi, pleural rub, and gurgling sounds). In total, it con-
tains 210 audio recordings categorized into three primary groups: 50 heart sound
recordings, 50 lung sound recordings, and 110 recordings of mixed heart and lung
sounds.

66 CHAPTER 3. LITERATURE REVIEW
3.1.3 Automated Respiratory Sound Analysis Tasks
Research on the automated analysis of respiratory sounds encompasses a diverse
range of tasks to improve the diagnosis and monitoring of respiratory conditions.
With the development of datasets with a large number of samples and subjects in re-
cent years, as described in subsection 3.1.2, a wide range of machine-learning-based
methods for multiple applications as well as different signal processing approaches
have been developed to support automated analysis of respiratory sound. Some of
the most common tasks are: 1) respiratory disease classification; 2) ARS event clas-
sification; 3) ARS classification; 4) ARS event detection; 5) respiratory cycle/phase
detection (inspiration and expiration); 6) respiratory airflow estimation.
As discussed in section 2.2, clinicians resort to multiple techniques and methods
to diagnose subjects with respiratory diseases, namely, lung auscultation, spirome-
try, and medical imaging, among others. Audio-based respiratory disease differential
diagnosis, or screening, can be formulated as a classification task, with the input of
respiratory sounds and output of a categorical prediction for the trained respiratory
conditions. Audio signals are time series characterized by temporal features and
spectral features in the transferred spectrograms (or other time-frequency represen-
tations). These features can be either explicitly utilized by traditional classifiers or
implicitly explored by deep learning models. These classification tasks might also
involve pre-processing the audio signals before feeding them into machine-learning
models to remove noise. A typical automatic audio-based respiratory condition
screening system development pipeline is illustrated in Figure 3.1. In the literature,
this pathology-driven classification has been addressed by performing the classifica-
tion of complete respiratory sound audio recordings or by solely classifying chunks
of complete audio recordings, such as isolated breathing cycles (namely, using the
RSD). Table 3.2 summarizes some of the most relevant works in the literature in
this classification task.
Figure 3.1: Generic respiratory diseases audio-based diagnosis pipeline overview (image
from [120]).
Automatic classification, detection, and segmentation of ARS are pivotal tasks
in respiratory sound analysis, which aims to enhance the accuracy and efficiency
of diagnosing and monitoring lung diseases. Adventitious sounds such as wheezes,

3.1. RESPIRATORY SOUND ANALYSIS 67
crackles, and stridor are abnormal breath sounds that provide key diagnostic clues
for respiratory conditions like asthma, COPD, and pneumonia, among others. While
ARS are not exclusive to subjects that suffer from respiratory diseases, their presence
is usually highly correlated with such diseases. Thus, they are extremely relevant
bio-markers to assess respiratory health.
The classification of ARS events is a classification task in which the main ob-
jective is to categorize these sounds into distinct types/classes, leveraging machine
learning algorithms that are trained on annotated datasets. In these types of prob-
lems, the ARS are already previously segmented; that is, the classification models
receive as inputs the respiratory signals within specific temporal instants, which
contains the event of interest to be classified. In the IEEE BioCAS 2023 Grand
Challenge on Respiratory Sound Classification for the SPRSound Dataset [114], one
of the tasks is precisely the classification of previously segmented ARS. Table 3.3
summarizes some of the most relevant works in the literature in this classification
task.
Similarly to the task of ARS event classification, the task of ARS classification
is also defined as a classification task in which the objective is to assign a specific
class to a sample respiratory sound. However, in this case, the ARS event samples
are not previously isolated nor segmented. That is, the temporal onset and offset
of the ARS events is not known. Instead, in this case of ARS classification, the
respiratory sound samples can be isolated respiratory cycles, a small chunk of a
certain respiratory sound signal, or even the complete respiratory sound recording
with multiple respiratory cycles. In the literature, this problem is one of the most
commonly addressed. In fact, it started to be greatly studied ever since the ICBHI
2017 Challenge [105], which aimed to determine whether a specific respiratory cycle
(previously isolated/segmented) was normal or contained crackles and or wheezes.
Also, in the IEEE BioCAS 2023 Grand Challenge on Respiratory Sound Classifi-
cation for the SPRSound Dataset [114], one of the tasks was to classify complete
respiratory sound recording for the presence/absence of ARS. Table 3.4 summarizes
some of the most relevant works in the literature in this classification task.
Unlike in the static classification of previously isolated ARS, the task of ARS
event detection involves locating and classifying specific sounds in complete audio
recordings by determining their temporal onset and offset. It involves detecting
the presence of certain sounds (ARS), determining their duration, and categorizing
them into predefined classes. The process typically includes signal processing, fea-
ture extraction, and applying machine learning models. The usual approach for this
problem is supervised learning with sound event classes defined in advance. The
complexity associated with such tasks is significantly higher than the static classifi-
cation tasks described in the previous paragraphs. The main reasons for the added
complexity can be summarized in the following points:
• ARS are rare events (in a typical respiratory sound database, with several hours

68 CHAPTER 3. LITERATURE REVIEW
of recordings, ARS only make up for a small percentage of the total recording
time)
• Typical noise in respiratory sound recordings can partially mask ARS
• Respiratory sound recordings can contain several confounding sounds. For in-
stance, voice, beep noises from machines in hospital settings, and other types
of sounds with harmonic-like characteristics can be confounded with CAS. Con-
versely, noises generated by the rubbing of the recording devices (e.g., digital
stethoscope) against the human body and other percussive-like sounds can be
confounded with DAS
• ARS, both CAS and DAS can vary widely from subject to subject and present
significantly different characteristics
Based on the above-mentioned reasons, it can be understood why it can be hard
to develop for the detection of ARS, especially when considering machine-learning-
based models with good generalization capability. Table 3.5 summarizes some of the
most relevant works in the literature in this classification task.
Besides the analysis of ARSs, the analysis of the breathing cycles and respira-
tory airflow are also very important to assess respiratory function. The automated
respiratory sound analysis literature has also widely explored these two tasks. Each
complete breathing cycle comprises an inspiratory phase followed by an expiratory
phase. Within these phases lies important information for evaluating the health
of the respiratory system, which can be located during inspiration or expiration.
Monitoring breathing patterns has critical importance in a wide range of clinical
applications, including intensive care monitoring, anesthesia administration, reha-
bilitation, physiotherapy sessions, and monitoring of sleep apnea as well as during
cardiac and pulmonary examinations [121]. Abnormal breathing rates and changes
in the breathing rate are among the earliest indicators of physiological instability.
Clinicians are interested not only in the presence or absence of adventitious sounds
but also in their location within the breathing cycle, whether inspiratory or expi-
ratory, and whether occurring early, mid, or late in that breathing phase. Such
information holds relevant clinical importance as these timing factors can indicate
the correct diagnosis, and changes in timing or an increase in the portion of the
phase where added sounds are found can indicate disease progression [121, 122].
Airflow measurements can also differentiate obstructive from restrictive pulmonary
disorders, characterize severity, and measure responses to therapy.
While breathing rate (breaths per minute) and breathing phase (inspiration and
expiration) can easily be identified using simple contact transducers and smart-
phone apps, clinicians investigating cardio-vascular conditions usually start their
medical examination with manual auscultation using a stethoscope. As for res-
piratory airflow, the standard methods for its measurement are pneumotachogra-
phy and spirometry. Despite such methods being well established and validated

3.1. RESPIRATORY SOUND ANALYSIS 69
in clinical practice, they suffer from significant drawbacks that hinder their effec-
tiveness, namely outside the clinical environment and telemonitoring applications.
For instance, pneumotachography requires a complex setup, and spirometry highly
depends on patient cooperation. Therefore, the ability to estimate the breathing
rate/phase and respiratory airflow directly from respiratory sound recorded with a
stethoscope presents significant advantages. It can be easily integrated into existing
and trusted technology with the clinical practice and deployed in remote applica-
tions. Table 3.6 summarizes some of the most relevant works in the literature in
this task.

3.1. RESPIRATORY SOUND ANALYSIS 89
3.1.4 Challenges, Open issues, and Future Perspectives
Although respiratory sound recording has been an active and growing area of re-
search activity and development for more than 30 years, it has yet to be established
as a tool for clinical diagnosis. The automated analysis and processing of respi-
ratory sounds faces several open issues, primarily related to data quality, model
generalization capability, and clinical validation.
Despite the progressive interest in the collection of respiratory sounds, one of
the main concerns is the need for more standardization in the recording process
and the evaluation of the developed methodologies, as different approaches have
been reported in the literature [38,44]. This point is particularly relevant regarding
the positions that should be used to record the respiratory sound or the type of
maneuvers that should be performed while recording. With the development of
more efficient methods of recording of breath sounds, multi-sensor arrays are also
being designed to provide better coverage and allow for multi-channel algorithmic
processing [105, 106, 168–172]. Another challenge is the lack of standardized, large-
scale, annotated databases of respiratory sounds, which are essential for training
robust machine learning models. Several of the newer open-access respiratory sound
databases presented in subsection 3.1.2 suffer from annotation errors, as is the case
with the RSD, HF databases, and SPRSound databases. Such errors will not only
introduce bias in the training process of the machine learning models but will also
affect the evaluation.
Several factors can affect the automated models’ generalization capability. One
major issue is related to the variability and quality of recorded sounds, which can be
affected by background noise, recording device inconsistencies and variability, and
patient movement. These factors complicate accurate signal acquisition, requiring
sophisticated noise reduction and preprocessing techniques that can work reliably
across diverse environments. Another challenge is the complexity and non-stationary
nature of respiratory sounds themselves. Respiratory sounds vary significantly with
patient-specific factors such as anatomy, age, and disease state, making it difficult
for models to generalize across populations. As pointed out in a study to estimate
the dimensionless respiratory airflow [167], models trained in a specific recording
device tend to generalize poorly when tested on data recorded with other devices.
One of the most promising areas for respiratory sound recording development
is wireless recording in wearable devices [173, 174]. This type of sensor is particu-
larly suitable for deployment in telemedicine and remote monitoring applications.
These applications can be of two kinds, namely asynchronous and synchronous. In
asynchronous telemedicine, data are collected and sent electronically to a medical
professional for further analysis and diagnosis. Synchronous telemedicine is real-time
and can be interactive. Moreover, in synchronous applications, audiovisual technol-
ogy may enable distant examination. There have been reports of self-monitoring

90 CHAPTER 3. LITERATURE REVIEW
of asthma and COPD patients at home using telemedicine tools, and the results
were accurate and satisfactory, without affecting the quality of care received by
these patients [175, 176]. Similarly, with the advances in smartphone and connec-
tivity technology, wireless stethoscopes working with mobile phones have also been
developed [177]. As technological development progresses, more of these devices
are likely to exist. The constant miniaturization of the sensors and the push to-
wards personal health monitoring solutions will be two driving factors, alongside
the worldwide adoption of wearable monitoring systems. Prospects also include
the development of low-power, portable, and wearable devices capable of continu-
ous monitoring, which could facilitate early detection of respiratory deterioration in
home and remote settings.
In conclusion, electronic auscultation coupled with computerized lung sound
analysis can potentially improve the diagnostic yield of pulmonary disorders, both
in clinical and research settings [44]. However, more studies are required to address
standardization of methodology and analytical methods across studies. This point
is particularly important so that different studies can be fairly and appropriately
compared. Therefore, these approaches face significant challenges before achieving
clinical acceptance, primarily due to a need for standardized clinical trials and a lack
of diagnostic validity. Notwithstanding, with further research, improvements in sen-
sor technology, and clinical validation studies, automated respiratory sound analysis
can transform respiratory healthcare by making it more accessible, objective, and
proactive.
3.2 Electrical Impedance Tomography (EIT)
Researchers across several industries and areas have used EIT because of its low-
cost and no-radiation properties. Although EIT has been used in industrial and
geophysical applications since 1930, the first steps towards its application in the
medical field were only taken in the early 1980s by the Sheffield group [178]. Their
system, the APT system mk-1, became the most widespread EIT system applied
successfully in many clinical applications, and it is considered a reference mark as it
forms the basis of many of the newly developed EIT systems. The system comprises
16 electrodes, and the current injected has only one frequency (50 kHz), which comes
from a single current source. Many other systems have been developed since then,
and the EIT research area has constantly expanded. Despite being mostly adopted in
clinical environments, EIT has also been gaining progressively more attention within
the academic community, leading to the further development of the technology.

3.2. ELECTRICAL IMPEDANCE TOMOGRAPHY (EIT) 91
3.2.1 EIT Reconstruction
The EIT reconstruction process involves three components: the data, the main
model (geometry, position of the electrodes), and the reconstruction method (for-
ward and inverse problem). EIT reconstruction algorithms are an essential part
of the data processing pipeline, as seen in chapter 2. Their function is to map
and transform the measured voltages on the electrodes to generate a cross-sectional
image of the impedance values. In chest EIT, these will produce a cross-sectional
2D/3D image of the lungs and the respective bioimpedance distribution.
To perform EIT image reconstruction, three sub-categories of techniques can be
used: frequency difference, absolute imaging, and time difference. Difference imag-
ing was the first type being developed by the Sheffield group [178]. It consists of
measuring two different data sets at different times or frequencies. These are then
subtracted (difference EIT) or subtracted and then divided (normalized difference
EIT) by a reference set of measurements corresponding to the background conduc-
tivity. The resulting images translate to changes in the background conductivity,
representing a given physiological parameter. The other type of image reconstruction
algorithm is called absolute imaging since it produces images containing the body’s
absolute conductivity or permittivity map. It is technologically more challenging,
as the contact impedance of the electrodes plays an important role and cannot be
accurately assessed when concerning a clinical application. These techniques rely
on a computer-based model, the mesh, of the subject/object of interest. This model
then simulates the estimated electrical potential distributions for the injected cur-
rent patterns [71]. Furthermore, EIT image reconstruction algorithms can also be
divided into two categories, deterministic and stochastic [71].
In summary, the EIT problem can be divided into two: the forward problem,
which calculates the electrical potentials in the boundary using an initial estimation
of the conductivity/resistivity distribution, and the inverse problem, which recon-
structs the conductivity/resistivity distribution based on the electrical potentials
measured in the boundary, through the use of a mathematical procedure such as the
Gauss-Newton Method, for instance [179]. The first step to obtain an EIT image is
the data collection from the subject’s body area under examination, followed by the
forward problem [71]. Current or voltage is applied to the body, and the resulting
current or voltage is calculated. Typically, the current is applied from an alternat-
ing current source to a particular pair of electrodes, and the measuring voltages are
calculated with the remaining electrodes [71].
Many techniques can be used in current-injecting electrodes, voltage-measuring
electrodes, and current patterns. Currently, some of the most common methods
to inject current into the electrodes are the Adjacent Drive method, the Opposite
method, and the Cross method [71, 82]. In Figure 3.2, the placement of electrodes
around the human torso and the pairs of electrodes that will inject and measure

92 CHAPTER 3. LITERATURE REVIEW
the electrical currents can be observed. In this schematic, current is injected using
electrodes 1 and 16, while the remaining ones are used to measure the voltage values.
This process is repeated for the current applied between all the adjacent electrode
pairs around the body. Hence, for a system with 16 electrodes, for example, one
data frame has the current applied from 16 positions, and each position has 13
voltage measurements. The resulting 16 ×13 = 208 values, also called a frame, are
used to reconstruct one cross-sectional EIT image. Besides the 16 electrodes, an
extra electrode is used as the reference electrode, attached to a central point on
the chest of the subject investigation/recording. The reference electrode ensures
that all measurements at different electrode pairs are referenced to the same electric
potential.
20|
PulmoVista 500 determines the distribution of intra-thoracic bioimpedance  
by applying a known alternating current “I1” to a first pair of electrodes  
and measuring the resulting surface potentials “Vn” at the remaining  
13 electrode pairs (Fig. 11). Applying Ohm’s law, the bioelectrical impedance 
between the injecting and the measuring electrode pairs is determined from 
the known applied current and the measured voltages. Subsequently, the 
adjacent electrode pair is used for the next current application and another  
13 voltage measurements are performed. The location of the injecting and 
measuring electrode pairs successively rotates around the entire thorax.  
One complete rotation creates voltage profiles at 16 electrode positions, each 
consisting of 13 voltage measurements (Fig. 13). The resulting 208 values, 
also called a frame, are used to reconstruct one cross-sectional EIT image.
Please see Appendix II for more details about bioimpedance measurements.
