Chapter 5
Conclusions
Contents
5.1 Summary and Main Contributions . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 183
5.2 Perspectives and Future Research . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 185
I
n this chapter, a generic overview of the work carried out in this thesis is
presented, as well as a summary of the contributions derived from it. Building
on these and considering the problems still open in the field, we draw some
of the possible lines of research to advance the automated respiratory health
monitoring field.
5.1 Summary and Main Contributions
At the beginning of this dissertation, our general goal was to develop methodologies
for the automated monitoring of respiratory health and function. To do so, we based
our approach on the development of machine learning-based pipelines using respi-
ratory sound and Electrical Impedance Tomography (EIT) on three major topics:
(1) automated analysis and classification of adventitious respiratory sounds, (2) dif-
ferential diagnosis of respiratory diseases, and (3) dimensionless respiratory airflow
estimation. Based on the initial goal, our main contributions can be summarized in
the following points:
• Compilation of the first publicly available bimodal (with respiratory sound and
EIT) database for the differential diagnosis of respiratory disease - Bimodal
Repository of Auscultation Coupled with Electrical Impedance Thoracic Signals
(BRACETS). This also includes the compilation of the first publicly available
extensive database on human chest EIT
183

184 CHAPTER 5. CONCLUSIONS
• Proposal of machine-learning based pipelines using EIT in the context of the
differential diagnosis of respiratory diseases
• Proposal of new EIT derived features (impedance curve correlations)
• Proposal of state-of-the-art algorithms for the automated analysis of Adventi-
tious Respiratory Sounds (ARS) (in static ARS events classification and in ARS
classification)
• Proposal of a new respiratory airflow concept based on ARS Global Impedance
Curve (GIC) (dimensionless respiratory airflow)
• Proposal of a Deep Learning (DL) model in the context of an airflow estimation-
related problem
In real-world scenarios, integrating multiple machine learning-based algorithms
to monitor different aspects of respiratory function, such as the ones developed
throughout this thesis, can revolutionize respiratory healthcare, especially in man-
aging chronic diseases such as several respiratory ones. With such an approach,
a comprehensive system can be created that provides real-time alerts to patients
and healthcare providers when a combination of abnormal patterns is detected, in-
dicating potential respiratory deterioration. Such an integrated system would be
particularly useful in remote settings where regular access to healthcare is limited,
enabling continuous and continued monitoring, early diagnosis, and timely interven-
tion without requiring frequent hospital visits. For instance, in subjects suffering
from asthma, the period before an acute crisis is usually preceded by a significant
increase in wheezing sounds. Such biomarkers could be tracked by automated al-
gorithms and used to adjust medication and avoid acute crises. Moreover, these
systems could be valuable in telemedicine platforms, providing clinicians with real-
time insights into a patient’s respiratory health, facilitating personalized treatment,
and improving overall outcomes.
Ultimately, we believe our research will significantly contribute to ongoing initia-
tives to advance the use of artificial intelligence techniques in automated respiratory
function assessment, particularly using respiratory sound and EIT. We aim to sub-
stantially improve the speed and efficacy of respiratory disease detection and man-
agement, primarily through the algorithmic deployment in wearable devices capable
of recording biosignals used in the context of remote or clinical settings. The deploy-
ment of such technology in wearable devices enhances the capability for continuous
and continuated, real-time monitoring in remote settings, offering significant ben-
efits for patients with chronic respiratory conditions and those in resource-limited
regions. As the technology matures, integrated respiratory monitoring systems will
play an increasingly vital role in preventive care, early diagnosis, and personalized
treatment of respiratory diseases.

5.2. PERSPECTIVES AND FUTURE RESEARCH 185
5.2 Perspectives and Future Research
Automated Monitoring of Respiratory Function: Are we there yet?
In recent years, there has been a significant push towards developing automated
methodologies to monitor respiratory function and overall respiratory system health,
namely with the differential diagnosis of respiratory diseases and tracking respiratory-
specific biomarkers. This is true for both respiratory sound and EIT. On the res-
piratory sound side, this progress has been fueled by the compilation of several
open-access databases on respiratory sound, as presented in subsection 3.1.2, which
led to an increasing number of studies published in the literature on the automated
analysis of these sounds. The publication of such databases had allowed the explo-
ration of primarily data-driven approaches for analyzing respiratory sounds, mainly
DL-based, which were essentially unexplored before as there were no publicly avail-
able sizeable databases. However, one of the leading promoters of the development
of such technologies to monitor respiratory health was, in fact, the COVID-19 pan-
demic. During this pandemic, primarily due to sanitary reasons, methodologies that
could help monitor patients remotely and contactless through the analysis of res-
piratory sound and cough have seen a massive gain in popularity, as one of their
main advantages was the fact that they could help lower the levels of exposure from
healthcare professionals. In turn, this has also led to a greater interest in the analysis
of generic respiratory sounds, not specifically COVID-related.
Automated monitoring reduces the reliance on subjective clinical assessments
and offers a scalable solution for the long-term management of respiratory condi-
tions, particularly in home-based or resource-limited settings. Despite the significant
developments that have been made in recent years towards a future where we can
have “AI Doctors” as our personal pulmonologists, capable of tracking and moni-
toring our overall respiratory health, such technologies are still not a reality as of
today due to several key challenges.
One of the main difficulties associated with the development of methodologies
for automated monitoring resides in one of the first steps usually required for such
pipelines: collecting and labeling large, high-quality datasets of biosignals. Develop-
ing accurate machine learning models requires representative, high-quality datasets,
and compiling such datasets is a very expensive, time-consuming, and resource-
consuming task. Apart from data collection, there is also the need for data an-
notation, which, again, is a resource-intensive task requiring high domain-specific
knowledge. In fact, one of the most significant limiting factors related to the perfor-
mance of supervised machine learning models is the quality of the annotated data
used in their training. Inaccurate or inconsistent labels can mislead the learning
algorithm, causing the models to make incorrect assumptions or produce wrong
outputs. Poor labels will also affect the evaluation of the models while developing

186 CHAPTER 5. CONCLUSIONS
them. In respiratory sound analysis, the issue of inaccurate and inconsistent ARS
annotation has been observed in several of the most relevant databases identified
in subsection 3.1.2, namely the Respiratory Sound Database (RSD), HF Lung V1,
HF Lung V2, HF Tracheal V1, and SPRSound databases.
Associated with the great difficulty in compiling large databases, there is also the
lack of clinical validation of the developed approaches. Most automated methodolo-
gies for respiratory function assessment in the literature, especially in the respiratory
sound processing area, are mostly developed solely within the academic context,
without any validation in a real clinical context. Conducting large-scale clinical
trials is expensive and resource-intensive, often requiring cross-institution collabora-
tion, which brings logistical challenges in securely coordinating data collection and
sharing. Overall, the challenges of patient variability, data collection complexity,
regulatory requirements, and ethical considerations make clinical validation of auto-
mated methods for respiratory monitoring complex and resource-intensive. Besides,
there are also concerns regarding user safety in the early stages of the development of
such systems. Successfully conducting such clinical validation studies requires over-
coming these obstacles to demonstrate that the technology is accurate and suitable
for real-world use across diverse, varied populations.
Another significant difficulty associated with adopting automated methodologies
to monitor respiratory function, especially in deploying wearable devices for remote
monitoring applications, is the development of technologies that can accurately and
consistently capture data in real-world, uncontrolled environments. Lower quality or
compromised recorded signals translate to poorer performance or misleading results
from the monitoring algorithms. Issues like sensor capability/sensitivity, reliability,
battery life, and user compliance also present challenges. For instance, although the
wearable vest developed under the scope of the Wearable Electronics for Effective
Lung Monitoring (WELMO) was able to capture good quality audible respiratory
sound signals as well as good EIT signals, in a pilot study with healthy subjects,
there were also several cases where the recorded signals were not of good quality
[172]. Besides the points mentioned above, regulatory approvals for medical artificial
intelligence systems are very strict, as these technologies must meet rigorous safety
and accuracy standards before they can be widely deployed.
Lastly, the “black box” nature of many advanced artificial intelligence mod-
els, such as deep learning, makes it difficult to provide the interpretability and
transparency required for clinical decision-making, limiting trust among healthcare
providers. The lack of such properties will inherently limit the adoption of auto-
mated methods in clinical practice.
Despite technological advancements in automated processing algorithms and
recording equipment, challenges related to data variability, device sensitivity, model
accuracy, reliability, and interpretability still need to be addressed to ensure the
adoption of automated respiratory function monitoring techniques. Overcoming

5.2. PERSPECTIVES AND FUTURE RESEARCH 187
these challenges will require further technological refinement, larger datasets, and
more robust validation, especially in clinical environments.
Where can we go next?
In generic terms, in the future, researchers will have to keep developing their meth-
ods to achieve higher performances and higher degrees of reliability, especially in
the area of automated processing of ARS. ARS are some of the most important
biomarkers for respiratory health because they provide critical information about
the presence and severity of abnormalities within the respiratory system. These
sounds often indicate underlying issues like airway obstruction, fluid accumulation,
or inflammation, all common in multiple respiratory conditions. These sounds can
also change over time in response to treatment or disease progression, making them
useful for diagnosis, ongoing monitoring, and treatment evaluation. ARS classifi-
cation and event detection are still ongoing problems in the literature, as current
methodologies still present some relatively poor metrics when it comes to their eval-
uation.
With the current trend of compiling newer and bigger repositories of respira-
tory sounds, researchers will have access to larger and more varied quantities of
data. They might explore newer and more complex models, especially newer state-
of-the-art DL architectures, that leverage considerable amounts of data. Given the
difficulty associated with the labeling of ARS, unsupervised and few-shot machine
learning methods might also be a promising approach. Few-shot learning is a ma-
chine learning framework in which an Artificial Intelligence (AI) model learns to
make accurate predictions by training on a very small number of labeled exam-
ples. On the other hand, unsupervised learning in artificial intelligence is a type of
machine learning that learns from data without human supervision. Unsupervised
machine learning models are given unlabeled data and allowed to discover patterns
and insights without explicit guidance or instruction. The two approaches offer a
way to minimize the impact associated with data processing and curation while
also helping deal with the resource-intensive nature of such processes and the wrong
annotations of the data.
One of the primary objectives of the current thesis was to study the potential
of combining respiratory sound and EIT for a better characterization of respira-
tory function. While we have studied the potential of merging these two sources
by combining features extracted from each signal at the feature level for the differ-
ential diagnosis of respiratory diseases (subsection 4.3.1), which led to an increase
in classification performance, we wanted to conduct further studies regarding the
complementarity of the two biosignals. However, despite our intention to study this
complementarity, we could not have the BRACETS database annotated by health-
care professionals regarding the presence of ARS in the respiratory sound record-

188 CHAPTER 5. CONCLUSIONS
ings. Due to COVID-19-related lockdowns, the database collection took significantly
longer than anticipated, which would also impose several constraints related to data
annotation.
The most concrete case in which we were interested was the study of how EIT
could be helpful to complement the characterization of ARS. One of the most imme-
diate ways this could be achieved is through the characterization of the respiratory
phase in which ARS occur since the temporal onsets of ARS can be determined
through the respiratory sound and their phase through EIT. This information is
clinically relevant as different occurrence timings (i.e., late inspiratory, early ex-
piratory) can be associated with different respiratory diseases [56, 122]. Another
concrete case we wanted to study was the lung function of areas of interest where
ARS were detected. For instance, if ARS were detected during auscultation in a
specific point over the left region of the body, we were interested to see if there were
any manifestations of altered lung function on the left lung that could be tracked
with EIT-derived measures.
Besides, incorporating multiple automated methodologies for the monitoring and
diagnosing of respiratory diseases represents a significant step forward in respiratory
healthcare. By combining diverse data streams and leveraging advanced machine
learning models, these integrated systems provide a more comprehensive, accurate,
and reliable assessment of respiratory health. Therefore, leveraging the use of the
bimodal database compiled in this thesis, research might develop new ways and
methods to combine the two sources.
Current automated methods to monitor respiratory function focus primarily on
an “instant snapshot” approach, where the patient is evaluated at a specific point in
time. Databases that track subjects over extended periods, rather than relying on
single-time recordings, are crucial for understanding the dynamic nature of respira-
tory function and accurately monitoring disease progression. Respiratory conditions,
such as asthma or Chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD), often present
daily symptom variations, or even within the same day due to factors like environ-
mental conditions, physical activity, or disease exacerbations. Long-span recordings
provide insights into these fluctuations by capturing data over time, allowing for a
more comprehensive understanding of each patient’s unique profile.
Longitudinal data can also reveal early signs of deterioration or subtle changes in
respiratory patterns and function that single recordings might miss, supporting early
intervention and better managing chronic conditions. For instance, a patient suffer-
ing from a chronic respiratory disease might start to produce ARS more frequently
in a certain period before an exacerbation. Such changes might also be captured
by regional lung function changes by EIT. For automated systems, access to data
collected over larger periods enables the development of predictive algorithms that
are better able to detect gradual changes and forecast future respiratory risks. This
also helps mitigate problems such as overfitting in machine learning models. It will

5.2. PERSPECTIVES AND FUTURE RESEARCH 189
make the models more reliable and robust, as they learn from a more diverse range
of patient states rather than being limited to snapshots that may not reflect typical
or clinically relevant patterns. Therefore, the design of studies aiming at follow-up
data collection over an extended period is one clear future line of research as it is
currently not being explored in the literature. Moreover, it will also allow researchers
to study data drift variations over time. However, collecting data at larger scales
over larger periods will bring additional challenges, namely related to data curation
and annotation, which will represent two very resource-intensive tasks.
Another area that holds great promise in automated respiratory sound analysis
is smartphone-based auscultation. Remote monitoring strategies can play an impor-
tant role in controlling symptoms, improving patients’ quality of life, and detecting
adverse events associated with significant morbidity [201]. Despite lung auscultation
being a quick, inexpensive, and efficient way to assess the respiratory system and
help monitor overall respiratory status, auscultation with a stethoscope is commonly
performed by a professional physician during in-person visits. Therefore, there is
a need for suitable alternatives that support teleconsultation and telemonitoring
applications to empower families, especially in lower-income countries with fewer
resources.
With technological developments, smartphones have become more affordable,
and, currently, the ownership rate of such devices is relatively high, even in lower-
income countries (78% worldwide and 47% in low-income countries) [202]. More-
over, recent studies have shown that the use of a smartphone built-in microphone
for children’s auscultation is a feasible alternative to the use of a stethoscope, both
when physicians and when parents performed the auscultation, as ARS detected
over stethoscope recordings could also be detected over smartphone ones [201,203].
Considering this, developing automated methodologies to process and analyze res-
piratory sound recorded over a smartphone is a promising research area, as they can
be easily and widely deployed. Transfer learning techniques might also be considered
to adapt previous models trained on sounds recorded with electronic stethoscopes
(or similar devices) to adapt them to smartphone recordings.
Explainable AI also represents a critical future research direction in the area of
respiratory function monitoring and diagnosing. As these domains increasingly rely
on machine learning models for identifying patterns and supporting clinical decisions,
ensuring that these models are interpretable and their predictions are comprehen-
sible becomes critical. Understanding the rationale behind a model’s outcome is
essential for clinical validation, as it allows healthcare professionals to evaluate the
reliability and relevance of the results in the context of patient care. To do so,
researchers could focus on techniques such as Local Interpretable Model-Agnostic
Explanations (LIME), Shapley values, or rule-based machine learning algorithms,
for instance. This analysis is of particular relevance when considering the differen-
tial diagnosis of respiratory diseases. Moreover, transparent AI systems foster trust,

190 CHAPTER 5. CONCLUSIONS
facilitate the identification of potential biases, and enable clinicians to integrate AI
insights effectively into their workflows.
